\section{Carbon: The Backbone of
Complexity}\label{carbon-the-backbone-of-complexity}

Why carbon? - 4 valence electrons → 4 bonds - Can bond to itself →
chains and rings - Right size → stable bonds - Multiple bond types →
single, double, triple

\section{Hydrocarbons}\label{hydrocarbons}

Molecules of just carbon and hydrogen.

\subsection{Alkanes (Single Bonds)}\label{alkanes-single-bonds}

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}llll@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
Formula & Name & Structure & Notes \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
\(\text{CH}_4\) & Methane & Tetrahedral & Natural gas \\
\(\text{C}_2\text{H}_6\) & Ethane & Two tetrahedra & \\
\(\text{C}_3\text{H}_8\) & Propane & Chain & Fuel \\
\(\text{C}_4\text{H}_{10}\) & Butane & Chain & Lighter fluid \\
\(\text{C}_5\text{H}_{12}\) & Pentane & Chain & Solvent \\
\end{longtable}

General formula: \(\text{C}_n\text{H}_{2n+2}\)

\subsection{Alkenes (Double Bonds)}\label{alkenes-double-bonds}

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}lll@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
Formula & Name & Notes \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
\(\text{C}_2\text{H}_4\) & Ethylene & Planar, ripening agent \\
\(\text{C}_3\text{H}_6\) & Propene & Plastic precursor \\
\end{longtable}

General formula: \(\text{C}_n\text{H}_{2n}\)

\subsection{Alkynes (Triple Bonds)}\label{alkynes-triple-bonds}

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}lll@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
Formula & Name & Notes \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
\(\text{C}_2\text{H}_2\) & Acetylene & Linear, welding fuel \\
\(\text{C}_3\text{H}_4\) & Propyne & \\
\end{longtable}

General formula: \(\text{C}_n\text{H}_{2n-2}\)

\subsection{Aromatic Compounds}\label{aromatic-compounds}

Ring structures with alternating double bonds.

\begin{figure}

\centering{

\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{chapters/../../media/images/fig-benzene.png}}

}

\caption{\label{fig-benzene}Benzene ring structure: six carbons in a
hexagonal ring with alternating double bonds.}

\end{figure}%

Benzene (\(\text{C}_6\text{H}_6\)) features a six-membered ring with
resonance: electrons delocalize around the entire ring rather than
alternating between fixed single and double bonds.

\section{Functional Groups}\label{functional-groups}

The reactive parts of molecules.

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}llll@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
Group & Formula & Name & Example \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
-OH & R-OH & Alcohol & Ethanol \\
-CHO & R-CHO & Aldehyde & Formaldehyde \\
-COOH & R-COOH & Carboxylic acid & Acetic acid \\
\(-\text{NH}_2\) & \(\text{R-NH}_2\) & Amine & Methylamine \\
-SH & R-SH & Thiol & Methanethiol \\
C=O & R-CO-R' & Ketone & Acetone \\
-O- & R-O-R' & Ether & Diethyl ether \\
-COO- & R-COO-R' & Ester & Ethyl acetate \\
\end{longtable}

\subsection{Alcohols}\label{alcohols}

Hydroxyl group (-OH) on carbon:

\begin{figure}

\centering{

\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{chapters/../../media/images/fig-ethanol.png}}

}

\caption{\label{fig-ethanol}Ethanol molecule showing hydroxyl group.}

\end{figure}%

Ethanol (CH₃CH₂OH) is the ``drinking alcohol'' found in beer, wine, and
spirits.

\subsection{Acids and Bases}\label{acids-and-bases}

\textbf{Carboxylic acids} donate \(\text{H}^+\): R-COOH \(\to\)
R-COO\(^-\) + \(\text{H}^+\)

\textbf{Amines} accept \(\text{H}^+\): \(\text{R-NH}_2\) +
\(\text{H}^+\) \(\to\) \(\text{R-NH}_3^+\)

\section{Isomers}\label{isomers}

Same formula, different structure.

\subsection{Structural Isomers}\label{structural-isomers}

Different connectivity: \textbf{Butane} (CH₃-CH₂-CH₂-CH₃) is a straight
chain, while \textbf{Isobutane} (CH₃-CH(CH₃)-CH₃) is branched. Same
formula (C₄H₁₀), different structure.

\subsection{Stereoisomers}\label{stereoisomers}

Same connectivity, different spatial arrangement: - \textbf{Cis-trans}:
Around double bonds - \textbf{Enantiomers}: Mirror images (chirality)

\subsection{Chirality}\label{chirality}

A carbon with 4 different groups is chiral:

\begin{figure}

\centering{

\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{chapters/../../media/images/fig-chirality.png}}

}

\caption{\label{fig-chirality}Chirality: a carbon bonded to four
different groups has non-superimposable mirror images.}

\end{figure}%

Mirror images are not superimposable. This matters in biology: life uses
only one ``handedness'' of amino acids.

\section{Reactions}\label{reactions}

\subsection{Combustion}\label{combustion}

\(\text{CH}_4 + 2\text{O}_2 \to \text{CO}_2 + 2\text{H}_2\text{O}\) +
energy

\subsection{Substitution}\label{substitution}

One group replaces another.

\subsection{Addition}\label{addition}

Atoms add across double bond.

\subsection{Elimination}\label{elimination}

Double bond forms, atoms leave.

\section{Experiments}\label{experiments-6}

\subsection{Molecule Builder}\label{molecule-builder}

Construct organic molecules from atoms. See 3D geometries.

\subsection{Reaction Simulator}\label{reaction-simulator}

Watch reactions occur: bonds breaking, bonds forming.

\subsection{Isomer Explorer}\label{isomer-explorer}

Find all isomers for a given formula.

\section{Concepts}\label{concepts-6}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{hydrocarbon}: C and H only
\item
  \textbf{functional-group}: Reactive part of molecule
\item
  \textbf{isomer}: Same formula, different structure
\item
  \textbf{chirality}: Mirror-image asymmetry
\end{itemize}

\section{Transition}\label{transition-6}

Complex molecules are still small compared to the giants of chemistry.
Next, we explore macromolecules: proteins, polymers, and DNA.

\chapter{Macromolecules}\label{sec-macromolecules}

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, leftrule=.75mm, title=\textcolor{quarto-callout-warning-color}{\faExclamationTriangle}\hspace{0.5em}{Pedagogical Context {[}NOT DERIVED FROM FTD{]}}, bottomrule=.15mm, opacityback=0, coltitle=black, arc=.35mm, colframe=quarto-callout-warning-color-frame, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-warning-color!10!white, colback=white, titlerule=0mm, breakable, bottomtitle=1mm, toptitle=1mm, rightrule=.15mm, toprule=.15mm, left=2mm, opacitybacktitle=0.6]

This chapter provides \textbf{standard biochemistry context}. The
content describes known macromolecular structures for pedagogical
completeness. \textbf{No quantitative predictions} are derived from FTD
axioms at this scale.

\end{tcolorbox}

\emph{``The giants of the molecular world''}

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, leftrule=.75mm, title=\textcolor{quarto-callout-note-color}{\faInfo}\hspace{0.5em}{Key Revelation}, bottomrule=.15mm, opacityback=0, coltitle=black, arc=.35mm, colframe=quarto-callout-note-color-frame, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-note-color!10!white, colback=white, titlerule=0mm, breakable, bottomtitle=1mm, toptitle=1mm, rightrule=.15mm, toprule=.15mm, left=2mm, opacitybacktitle=0.6]

From simple building blocks, enormous structures emerge. Proteins fold,
DNA encodes, and polymers give materials their properties. Life is
macromolecular.

\end{tcolorbox}

\section{Proteins}\label{proteins}

Chains of amino acids that do everything in life.

\subsection{Amino Acids}\label{amino-acids}

20 standard amino acids, each with: - Amino group (\(-\text{NH}_2\)) -
Carboxyl group (-COOH) - R group (side chain: what makes each unique)

The general structure: \(\text{H}_2\text{N}\)-CH(R)-COOH, where R is the
side chain unique to each amino acid.

\subsection{Peptide Bonds}\label{peptide-bonds}

Amino acids link by condensation, releasing water and forming the
peptide bond (-CO-NH-) that chains amino acids together into proteins.

\subsection{Protein Structure}\label{protein-structure}

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}lll@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
Level & Description & Forces \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
Primary & Amino acid sequence & Covalent (peptide bonds) \\
Secondary & α-helix, β-sheet & Hydrogen bonds \\
Tertiary & 3D folding & All forces \\
Quaternary & Multiple chains & All forces \\
\end{longtable}

\subsection{Protein Functions}\label{protein-functions}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Enzymes}: Catalyze reactions
\item
  \textbf{Structural}: Collagen, keratin
\item
  \textbf{Transport}: Hemoglobin
\item
  \textbf{Immune}: Antibodies
\item
  \textbf{Signaling}: Hormones
\end{itemize}

\section{Nucleic Acids}\label{nucleic-acids}

Information storage molecules.

\subsection{DNA Structure}\label{dna-structure}

\begin{figure}

\centering{

\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{chapters/../../media/images/fig-dna-helix.png}}

}

\caption{\label{fig-dna-helix}DNA double helix structure with
sugar-phosphate backbone and base pairing.}

\end{figure}%

Two antiparallel sugar-phosphate backbones connected by base pairs: -
\textbf{A-T}: Adenine pairs with thymine via 2 hydrogen bonds -
\textbf{G-C}: Guanine pairs with cytosine via 3 hydrogen bonds

\subsection{Double Helix}\label{double-helix}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  10 base pairs per turn
\item
  3.4 nm pitch
\item
  Major and minor grooves
\item
  Antiparallel strands
\end{itemize}

\subsection{RNA}\label{rna}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Single-stranded (usually)
\item
  Uracil instead of thymine
\item
  Sugar is ribose (not deoxyribose)
\item
  Types: mRNA, tRNA, rRNA
\end{itemize}

\section{Polymers}\label{polymers}

Long chains of repeating units (monomers).

\subsection{Addition Polymers}\label{addition-polymers}

Monomers add without losing atoms:

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}lll@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
Monomer & Polymer & Use \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
Ethylene & Polyethylene & Plastic bags \\
Propylene & Polypropylene & Containers \\
Styrene & Polystyrene & Foam, packaging \\
Vinyl chloride & PVC & Pipes \\
\end{longtable}

Example: n(\(\text{CH}_2\text{=CH}_2\)) \(\to\)
\(-[-\text{CH}_2\text{-CH}_2-]_n-\) (polyethylene)

\subsection{Condensation Polymers}\label{condensation-polymers}

Monomers lose small molecule (usually water):

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}lll@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
Type & Example & Use \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
Polyester & PET & Bottles, fabric \\
Polyamide & Nylon & Fibers, fabric \\
Polyurethane & PU & Foam, coatings \\
\end{longtable}

\section{Carbohydrates}\label{carbohydrates}

Sugars and their polymers.

\subsection{Monosaccharides}\label{monosaccharides}

Simple sugars: glucose, fructose, galactose (all
\(\text{C}_6\text{H}_{12}\text{O}_6\))

\subsection{Disaccharides}\label{disaccharides}

Two sugars linked: - Sucrose = glucose + fructose - Lactose = glucose +
galactose - Maltose = glucose + glucose

\subsection{Polysaccharides}\label{polysaccharides}

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}lll@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
Name & Function & Source \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
Starch & Energy storage & Plants \\
Glycogen & Energy storage & Animals \\
Cellulose & Structure & Plant cell walls \\
Chitin & Structure & Fungi, arthropods \\
\end{longtable}

\section{Lipids}\label{lipids}

Not true polymers, but complex molecules.

\subsection{Fatty Acids}\label{fatty-acids}

Long hydrocarbon chain + carboxyl group:
\(\text{CH}_3(\text{CH}_2)_n\text{COOH}\)

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Saturated}: No double bonds, solid at room temperature
\item
  \textbf{Unsaturated}: Double bonds, liquid at room temperature
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Phospholipids}\label{phospholipids}

Basis of cell membranes:

\begin{figure}

\centering{

\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{chapters/../../media/images/fig-lipid-bilayer.png}}

}

\caption{\label{fig-phospholipid}Phospholipid structure: hydrophilic
head and hydrophobic tails.}

\end{figure}%

Each phospholipid has a hydrophilic head (phosphate group) and
hydrophobic tails (fatty acid chains). In water, they spontaneously form
bilayers with heads facing outward and tails facing inward.

\section{Experiments}\label{experiments-7}

\subsection{Protein Folding}\label{protein-folding}

Watch a chain of amino acids fold into its final structure.

\subsection{DNA Replication}\label{dna-replication}

Observe the double helix unzip and copy.

\subsection{Polymer Synthesis}\label{polymer-synthesis}

Build polymers from monomers. Compare properties.

\section{Concepts}\label{concepts-7}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{protein}: Amino acid polymer with complex structure
\item
  \textbf{nucleic-acid}: Information-carrying polymer (DNA, RNA)
\item
  \textbf{polymer}: Long chain of repeating units
\item
  \textbf{carbohydrate}: Sugar or sugar polymer
\end{itemize}

\section{Transition}\label{transition-7}

Macromolecules have complex properties. Now we zoom out to see how
molecules aggregate into bulk matter: the different states and their
transitions.

\part{States of Matter}

\chapter{States of Matter}\label{sec-states-matter}

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, leftrule=.75mm, title=\textcolor{quarto-callout-warning-color}{\faExclamationTriangle}\hspace{0.5em}{Pedagogical Context {[}NOT DERIVED FROM FTD{]}}, bottomrule=.15mm, opacityback=0, coltitle=black, arc=.35mm, colframe=quarto-callout-warning-color-frame, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-warning-color!10!white, colback=white, titlerule=0mm, breakable, bottomtitle=1mm, toptitle=1mm, rightrule=.15mm, toprule=.15mm, left=2mm, opacitybacktitle=0.6]

This chapter provides \textbf{standard thermodynamics context}. While
FTD proposes a mechanism for emergent thermodynamics (see callout
below), \textbf{no quantitative predictions} (melting points, heat
capacities) are derived from FTD axioms at this scale. The mechanisms
described are {[}CONJECTURE{]} - proposed correspondences, not rigorous
derivations.

\end{tcolorbox}

\emph{``Solid, liquid, gas: and beyond''}

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, leftrule=.75mm, title=\textcolor{quarto-callout-note-color}{\faInfo}\hspace{0.5em}{Key Revelation}, bottomrule=.15mm, opacityback=0, coltitle=black, arc=.35mm, colframe=quarto-callout-note-color-frame, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-note-color!10!white, colback=white, titlerule=0mm, breakable, bottomtitle=1mm, toptitle=1mm, rightrule=.15mm, toprule=.15mm, left=2mm, opacitybacktitle=0.6]

The same atoms behave completely differently depending on temperature
and pressure. Phase is about motion, not identity.

\end{tcolorbox}

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, leftrule=.75mm, title=\textcolor{quarto-callout-note-color}{\faInfo}\hspace{0.5em}{How FTD Proposes Thermodynamics {[}CONJECTURE{]}}, bottomrule=.15mm, opacityback=0, coltitle=black, arc=.35mm, colframe=quarto-callout-note-color-frame, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-note-color!10!white, colback=white, titlerule=0mm, breakable, bottomtitle=1mm, toptitle=1mm, rightrule=.15mm, toprule=.15mm, left=2mm, opacitybacktitle=0.6]

Temperature, pressure, and phase are \emph{proposed} to emerge in FTD:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Kinetic energy} = average squared velocity of manifested
  particles
\item
  \textbf{Temperature} emerges as a proxy:
  \(T_{\text{proxy}} = \langle|\mathbf{J}|^2\rangle / 3N\)
\item
  \textbf{Pressure} = rate of flux exchange at system boundaries
\item
  \textbf{Phase} = classification based on density, mobility, and
  correlation length
\end{itemize}

Standard thermodynamic laws (PV = nRT, entropy increase) emerge in the
continuum limit. For the rigorous derivation, see
THEORETICAL\_FOUNDATIONS Part IV: Statistical Mechanics.

\end{tcolorbox}

\section{The Four Primary States}\label{the-four-primary-states}

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}llll@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
State & Density & Order & Particle Motion \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
Solid & High & Long-range & Vibration in place \\
Liquid & High & Short-range & Flow, but touch \\
Gas & Low & None & Free movement \\
Plasma & Low & None & Ionized, free electrons \\
\end{longtable}

\begin{figure}

\centering{

\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{chapters/../../media/images/fig-states-of-matter-panels.png}}

}

\caption{\label{fig-states-of-matter}The four primary states of matter
and their characteristic properties.}

\end{figure}%

\section{Solid}\label{solid}

Particles locked in fixed positions.

\subsection{Properties}\label{properties-1}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Definite shape and volume
\item
  Incompressible
\item
  Particles vibrate around fixed points
\item
  Long-range order (crystalline) or short-range (amorphous)
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Types}\label{types}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Crystalline}: Regular lattice (salt, diamond)
\item
  \textbf{Amorphous}: No long-range order (glass, plastic)
\end{itemize}

\section{Liquid}\label{liquid}

Particles touching but mobile.

\subsection{Properties}\label{properties-2}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Definite volume, no definite shape
\item
  Nearly incompressible
\item
  Particles slide past each other
\item
  Short-range order only
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Unique Features}\label{unique-features}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Surface tension (inward force from cohesion)
\item
  Viscosity (resistance to flow)
\item
  Capillary action (climbing narrow tubes)
\end{itemize}

\section{Gas}\label{gas}

Particles far apart and free.

\subsection{Properties}\label{properties-3}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  No definite shape or volume
\item
  Highly compressible
\item
  Particles move randomly
\item
  No order
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Gas Laws}\label{gas-laws}

\textbf{Ideal Gas Law}: \[
PV = nRT
\]

where R = 8.314 J/(mol·K) is the ideal gas constant, P is pressure, V is
volume, n is the number of moles, and T is temperature.

\textbf{Kinetic Theory}: \[
\frac{3}{2}kT = \frac{1}{2}mv^2_{\text{avg}}
\]

Temperature IS average kinetic energy.

\section{Plasma}\label{plasma}

Ionized gas: electrons freed from atoms.

\subsection{Properties}\label{properties-4}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Electrically conductive
\item
  Responds to magnetic fields
\item
  Emits light
\item
  Most common state in the universe
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Where Found}\label{where-found}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Stars (99\% of visible matter)
\item
  Lightning
\item
  Neon signs
\item
  Fusion reactors
\end{itemize}

\section{Temperature in Simulation}\label{temperature-in-simulation}

Temperature emerges from particle motion. In FTD, we define a
\textbf{temperature proxy} that mirrors the kinetic theory result:

\[
T_{\text{proxy}} = \frac{\langle|\mathbf{J}|^2\rangle}{3N}
\]

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, leftrule=.75mm, title=\textcolor{quarto-callout-note-color}{\faInfo}\hspace{0.5em}{Dimensional Analysis}, bottomrule=.15mm, opacityback=0, coltitle=black, arc=.35mm, colframe=quarto-callout-note-color-frame, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-note-color!10!white, colback=white, titlerule=0mm, breakable, bottomtitle=1mm, toptitle=1mm, rightrule=.15mm, toprule=.15mm, left=2mm, opacitybacktitle=0.6]

In physical units, kinetic theory gives
\(\frac{3}{2}k_B T = \frac{1}{2}m\langle v^2\rangle\), so
\(T = \frac{m\langle v^2\rangle}{3k_B}\). In FTD natural units where
flux \(|J|\) serves as a momentum proxy and we set \(k_B = 1\), the
temperature proxy has dimensions of energy per particle. The factor of 3
accounts for three spatial dimensions (equipartition theorem).

\end{tcolorbox}

Higher average squared flux magnitude = higher temperature.

\section{Pressure in Simulation}\label{pressure-in-simulation}

Pressure from particle collisions with boundaries:

\[
P = \frac{F}{A} = \frac{\text{momentum transfer}}{\text{area} \times \text{time}}
\]

\section{Phase in the Lattice}\label{phase-in-the-lattice}

Phase determined by: 1. \textbf{Density}: Particles per region 2.
\textbf{Temperature proxy}: Average \textbar flux\textbar{} 3.
\textbf{Order parameter}: How correlated are neighbor positions?

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ determine\_phase(region):}
\NormalTok{    density }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ count\_particles(region) }\OperatorTok{/}\NormalTok{ region.volume}
\NormalTok{    temperature }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ average\_flux\_magnitude(region)}
\NormalTok{    order }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ correlation\_length(region)}

    \ControlFlowTok{if}\NormalTok{ density }\OperatorTok{\textgreater{}}\NormalTok{ HIGH }\KeywordTok{and}\NormalTok{ order }\OperatorTok{\textgreater{}}\NormalTok{ LONG\_RANGE:}
        \ControlFlowTok{return}\NormalTok{ SOLID}
    \ControlFlowTok{elif}\NormalTok{ density }\OperatorTok{\textgreater{}}\NormalTok{ HIGH }\KeywordTok{and}\NormalTok{ order }\OperatorTok{\textgreater{}}\NormalTok{ SHORT\_RANGE:}
        \ControlFlowTok{return}\NormalTok{ LIQUID}
    \ControlFlowTok{elif}\NormalTok{ density }\OperatorTok{\textless{}}\NormalTok{ LOW:}
        \ControlFlowTok{if}\NormalTok{ ionized(region):}
            \ControlFlowTok{return}\NormalTok{ PLASMA}
        \ControlFlowTok{else}\NormalTok{:}
            \ControlFlowTok{return}\NormalTok{ GAS}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\section{Experiments}\label{experiments-8}

\subsection{Heat and Cool}\label{heat-and-cool}

Start with solid. Add energy. Watch transition to liquid, then gas.

\subsection{Pressure Changes}\label{pressure-changes}

Compress a gas. See density increase, temperature rise.

\subsection{Phase Boundaries}\label{phase-boundaries}

Map the conditions where different phases exist.

\section{Concepts}\label{concepts-8}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{solid}: Fixed positions, long-range order
\item
  \textbf{liquid}: Touching, flowing, short-range order
\item
  \textbf{gas}: Separated, free motion
\item
  \textbf{plasma}: Ionized gas
\end{itemize}

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, leftrule=.75mm, title=\textcolor{quarto-callout-note-color}{\faInfo}\hspace{0.5em}{Chapter Summary}, bottomrule=.15mm, opacityback=0, coltitle=black, arc=.35mm, colframe=quarto-callout-note-color-frame, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-note-color!10!white, colback=white, titlerule=0mm, breakable, bottomtitle=1mm, toptitle=1mm, rightrule=.15mm, toprule=.15mm, left=2mm, opacitybacktitle=0.6]

\textbf{Matter exists in four primary phases}: solid (fixed positions,
long-range order), liquid (touching but mobile, short-range order), gas
(separated, no order), and plasma (ionized, responds to fields).
\textbf{Temperature emerges} as average kinetic flux: T\_proxy =
\(\langle|J|^2\rangle\)/3N. \textbf{Phase is determined} by density,
temperature proxy, and correlation length. The same atoms behave
completely differently depending on conditions: phase is about motion,
not identity. Plasma is the most common state in the universe (99\% of
visible matter is in stars).

\end{tcolorbox}

\section{Transition}\label{transition-8}

States exist in equilibrium under specific conditions. At the
boundaries, transitions occur. Next, we explore phase transitions:
melting, boiling, and more in Chapter~\ref{sec-phase-transitions}.

\item

  melting, boiling, and critical phenomena
\item
  \textbf{Chemical bonds}: Chapter~\ref{sec-chemical-bonds} explains how
  atoms connect in different states
\item
  \textbf{Exotic states}: Chapter~\ref{sec-exotic-states} explores
  superconductors and superfluids
\end{itemize}

\end{tcolorbox}

\chapter{Phase Transitions}\label{sec-phase-transitions}

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, leftrule=.75mm, title=\textcolor{quarto-callout-warning-color}{\faExclamationTriangle}\hspace{0.5em}{Pedagogical Context {[}NOT DERIVED FROM FTD{]}}, bottomrule=.15mm, opacityback=0, coltitle=black, arc=.35mm, colframe=quarto-callout-warning-color-frame, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-warning-color!10!white, colback=white, titlerule=0mm, breakable, bottomtitle=1mm, toptitle=1mm, rightrule=.15mm, toprule=.15mm, left=2mm, opacitybacktitle=0.6]

This chapter provides \textbf{standard thermodynamics context}. While
FTD proposes mechanisms for phase transitions (see callout below),
\textbf{no quantitative predictions} (critical temperatures, latent
heats) are derived from FTD axioms at this scale. The mechanisms
described are {[}CONJECTURE{]}.

\end{tcolorbox}

\emph{``When matter transforms''}

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, leftrule=.75mm, title=\textcolor{quarto-callout-note-color}{\faInfo}\hspace{0.5em}{Key Revelation}, bottomrule=.15mm, opacityback=0, coltitle=black, arc=.35mm, colframe=quarto-callout-note-color-frame, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-note-color!10!white, colback=white, titlerule=0mm, breakable, bottomtitle=1mm, toptitle=1mm, rightrule=.15mm, toprule=.15mm, left=2mm, opacitybacktitle=0.6]

Phase transitions happen at specific conditions: the critical points
where energy finally overcomes the forces holding a phase together.

\end{tcolorbox}

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, leftrule=.75mm, title=\textcolor{quarto-callout-note-color}{\faInfo}\hspace{0.5em}{How FTD Proposes Phase Transitions {[}CONJECTURE{]}}, bottomrule=.15mm, opacityback=0, coltitle=black, arc=.35mm, colframe=quarto-callout-note-color-frame, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-note-color!10!white, colback=white, titlerule=0mm, breakable, bottomtitle=1mm, toptitle=1mm, rightrule=.15mm, toprule=.15mm, left=2mm, opacitybacktitle=0.6]

Phase transitions in FTD are \emph{proposed} to arise from competition
between flux gradients:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Binding flux} holds structures together (attractive gradients)
\item
  \textbf{Kinetic flux} enables particle motion (momentum distribution)
\item
  \textbf{Critical point} occurs when kinetic flux overcomes binding
  flux
\item
  \textbf{Latent heat} = flux energy required to break neighbor
  correlations
\end{itemize}

The Clausius-Clapeyron equation emerges from flux balance at phase
boundaries.

\end{tcolorbox}

\section{The Phase Diagram}\label{the-phase-diagram}

\begin{figure}

\centering{

\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{chapters/../../media/images/fig-phase-diagram.png}}

}

\caption{\label{fig-phase-diagram}Phase diagram showing the three
primary phases of matter and their transitions.}

\end{figure}%

The phase diagram maps pressure versus temperature, revealing distinct
regions for solid, liquid, gas, and supercritical fluid.

\subsection{Key Points}\label{key-points}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Triple point}: All three phases coexist simultaneously
\item
  \textbf{Critical point}: Liquid-gas distinction vanishes; above this
  point lies the supercritical fluid region
\end{itemize}

\section{Transition Types}\label{transition-types}

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}lll@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
Transition & Direction & Energy \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
Melting & Solid \(\to\) Liquid & Absorb (\(\Delta H > 0\)) \\
Freezing & Liquid \(\to\) Solid & Release (\(\Delta H < 0\)) \\
Vaporization & Liquid \(\to\) Gas & Absorb \\
Condensation & Gas \(\to\) Liquid & Release \\
Sublimation & Solid \(\to\) Gas & Absorb \\
Deposition & Gas \(\to\) Solid & Release \\
Ionization & Gas \(\to\) Plasma & Absorb \\
Recombination & Plasma \(\to\) Gas & Release \\
\end{longtable}

\section{Latent Heat}\label{latent-heat}

Energy absorbed or released during transition without temperature
change.

\subsection{Heat of Fusion}\label{heat-of-fusion}

Energy to melt solid: \[
Q = m \times L_f
\]

\subsection{Heat of Vaporization}\label{heat-of-vaporization}

Energy to boil liquid: \[
Q = m \times L_v
\]

Usually \(L_v >> L_f\) because you must completely separate molecules.

\section{First-Order Transitions}\label{first-order-transitions}

Sharp boundaries, latent heat involved.

\subsection{Characteristics}\label{characteristics}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Discontinuity in first derivative of free energy
\item
  Coexisting phases at transition
\item
  Latent heat required
\item
  Examples: Melting, boiling, sublimation
\end{itemize}

\section{Second-Order Transitions}\label{second-order-transitions}

Continuous change, no latent heat.

\subsection{Characteristics}\label{characteristics-1}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Discontinuity in second derivative of free energy
\item
  No latent heat
\item
  Order parameter changes continuously
\item
  Examples: Ferromagnetic → paramagnetic, superconducting transition
\end{itemize}

\section{The Clausius-Clapeyron
Equation}\label{the-clausius-clapeyron-equation}

Describes the slope of phase boundaries:

\[
\frac{dP}{dT} = \frac{L}{T \Delta V}
\]

Where: - L = latent heat - ΔV = volume change

\subsection{Water's Anomaly}\label{waters-anomaly}

Ice is less dense than water (ΔV \textless{} 0), so melting curve has
negative slope. Higher pressure → lower melting point.

\section{Nucleation}\label{nucleation}

Transitions do not happen uniformly: they start at nucleation sites.

\subsection{Homogeneous Nucleation}\label{homogeneous-nucleation}

Random fluctuations create seeds of new phase. Rare.

\subsection{Heterogeneous Nucleation}\label{heterogeneous-nucleation}

Impurities or surfaces provide sites. Much more common.

\subsection{Supercooling/Superheating}\label{supercoolingsuperheating}

Without nucleation sites, matter can exist past transition temperature:
- Supercooled water: Liquid below 0°C - Superheated liquid: Liquid above
boiling point

\section{Critical Phenomena}\label{critical-phenomena}

Near the critical point: - Fluctuations become huge - Properties diverge
(critical opalescence) - No distinction between liquid and gas

Above the critical point: \textbf{supercritical fluid}: properties of
both liquid and gas.

\section{Cosmological Phase
Transitions}\label{cosmological-phase-transitions}

The early universe underwent phase transitions just like water, but at
vastly higher temperatures.

\subsection{The Electroweak Transition (T \textasciitilde{} 100
GeV)}\label{the-electroweak-transition-t-100-gev}

At T \textasciitilde{} 10¹⁵ K (100 GeV), the Higgs field condensed:

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}ll@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
Before & After \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
\(\langle H \rangle = 0\) & \(\langle H \rangle = 246\) GeV \\
W, Z massless & W, Z massive \\
EM and Weak unified & EM and Weak separate \\
\end{longtable}

\textbf{In FTD}: This is a \textbf{first-order} transition. It proceeds
via bubble nucleation: exactly like water boiling, but with the Higgs
condensate forming inside bubbles.

The bubble walls are where \textbf{baryogenesis} happens (see Chapter
10.4).

\subsection{The QCD Transition (T \textasciitilde{} 150
MeV)}\label{the-qcd-transition-t-150-mev}

At T \textasciitilde{} 10¹² K (150 MeV), quarks and gluons confined into
hadrons:

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}ll@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
Before & After \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
Quark-gluon plasma & Protons, neutrons, pions \\
Free quarks & Quarks confined in color-neutral combinations \\
\(\chi\) symmetry intact & \(\chi\) symmetry spontaneously broken \\
\end{longtable}

\textbf{In FTD}: This is a \textbf{crossover} (smooth transition), not
first-order. The flux configurations reorganize continuously rather than
via bubbles.

\subsection{Phase Transitions in FTD}\label{phase-transitions-in-ftd}

All phase transitions in FTD involve the flux field reorganizing:

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}lll@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
Transition & Flux Behavior & Order \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
Melting & Bond flux breaks, particles move freely & First-order \\
Boiling & Complete flux separation & First-order \\
Ferromagnetic & Flux alignments disorder & Second-order \\
Superconducting & Flux pairing (Cooper pairs) & Second-order \\
Electroweak & Higgs condensate forms & First-order \\
QCD & Color confinement & Crossover \\
\end{longtable}

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, leftrule=.75mm, title=\textcolor{quarto-callout-note-color}{\faInfo}\hspace{0.5em}{Universal Physics}, bottomrule=.15mm, opacityback=0, coltitle=black, arc=.35mm, colframe=quarto-callout-note-color-frame, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-note-color!10!white, colback=white, titlerule=0mm, breakable, bottomtitle=1mm, toptitle=1mm, rightrule=.15mm, toprule=.15mm, left=2mm, opacitybacktitle=0.6]

The same mathematics describes ice melting and the electroweak
transition: both involve an order parameter (flux configuration)
changing as temperature crosses a critical value.

\end{tcolorbox}

\section{Experiments}\label{experiments-9}

\subsection{Melting Point}\label{melting-point}

Heat a solid. Watch the transition. Measure temperature during phase
change.

\subsection{Triple Point}\label{triple-point}

Find conditions where solid, liquid, and gas coexist.

\subsection{Critical Point}\label{critical-point}

Approach the critical point. Watch the liquid-gas interface disappear.

\section{Implementation}\label{implementation-1}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ check\_phase\_transition(particle, neighbors):}
\NormalTok{    local\_temp }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ average\_flux\_magnitude(neighbors)}
\NormalTok{    local\_pressure }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ density(neighbors) }\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ local\_temp}

\NormalTok{    current\_phase }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ particle.phase}

    \CommentTok{\# Check transition conditions}
    \ControlFlowTok{if}\NormalTok{ current\_phase }\OperatorTok{==}\NormalTok{ SOLID:}
        \ControlFlowTok{if}\NormalTok{ local\_temp }\OperatorTok{\textgreater{}}\NormalTok{ melting\_point(particle.}\BuiltInTok{type}\NormalTok{):}
\NormalTok{            particle.phase }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ LIQUID}
\NormalTok{            absorb\_heat(particle, latent\_heat\_fusion)}

    \ControlFlowTok{elif}\NormalTok{ current\_phase }\OperatorTok{==}\NormalTok{ LIQUID:}
        \ControlFlowTok{if}\NormalTok{ local\_temp }\OperatorTok{\textgreater{}}\NormalTok{ boiling\_point(particle.}\BuiltInTok{type}\NormalTok{):}
\NormalTok{            particle.phase }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ GAS}
\NormalTok{            absorb\_heat(particle, latent\_heat\_vaporization)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\section{Concepts}\label{concepts-9}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{latent-heat}: Energy for phase change without temperature
  change
\item
  \textbf{nucleation}: Starting points for phase transitions
\item
  \textbf{triple-point}: Three phases coexist
\item
  \textbf{critical-point}: Liquid-gas distinction vanishes
\item
  \textbf{electroweak-transition}: First-order at T \textasciitilde{}
  100 GeV, Higgs condensation
\item
  \textbf{qcd-transition}: Crossover at T \textasciitilde{} 150 MeV,
  quark confinement
\item
  \textbf{order-parameter}: Quantity that changes at transition (flux
  configuration in FTD)
\end{itemize}

\section{Transition}\label{transition-9}

Normal phases cover most conditions. But at extremes, exotic states
emerge: superconductors, superfluids, and more in
Chapter~\ref{sec-exotic-states}.

\chapter{Exotic States}\label{sec-exotic-states}

\textbf{no quantitative predictions} (critical temperatures, order
parameters) are derived from FTD axioms at this scale. The mechanisms

\emph{``When matter does the impossible''}

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, leftrule=.75mm, title=\textcolor{quarto-callout-note-color}{\faInfo}\hspace{0.5em}{Key Revelation}, bottomrule=.15mm, opacityback=0, coltitle=black, arc=.35mm, colframe=quarto-callout-note-color-frame, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-note-color!10!white, colback=white, titlerule=0mm, breakable, bottomtitle=1mm, toptitle=1mm, rightrule=.15mm, toprule=.15mm, left=2mm, opacitybacktitle=0.6]

behaviors that defy everyday intuition. These exotic states reveal
quantum mechanics at macroscopic scales.

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, leftrule=.75mm, title=\textcolor{quarto-callout-note-color}{\faInfo}\hspace{0.5em}{How FTD Proposes Exotic States {[}CONJECTURE{]}}, bottomrule=.15mm, opacityback=0, coltitle=black, arc=.35mm, colframe=quarto-callout-note-color-frame, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-note-color!10!white, colback=white, titlerule=0mm, breakable, bottomtitle=1mm, toptitle=1mm, rightrule=.15mm, toprule=.15mm, left=2mm, opacitybacktitle=0.6]

Exotic quantum phases are \emph{proposed} to emerge when flux behavior
becomes macroscopically coherent:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Superconductivity} = Cooper pairs form when flux-mediated
  attraction overcomes thermal disruption; pairs are bosonic and
  condense into coherent flux state
\item
  \textbf{Superfluidity} = Bose-Einstein condensation of flux into a
  single macroscopic quantum state
\item
  \textbf{Bose-Einstein condensate} = many particles sharing identical
  flux configuration at T → 0
\item
  \textbf{Fermi degeneracy} = Pauli exclusion forces fermion flux into
  high-momentum states
\end{itemize}

These states reveal that the flux field, not individual particles, is
the fundamental reality.

\end{tcolorbox}

\section{Superconductivity}\label{superconductivity}

Below a critical temperature, some materials conduct electricity with
zero resistance.

\subsection{Properties}\label{properties-5}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Zero electrical resistance
\item
  Meissner effect (expels magnetic fields)
\item
  Persistent currents (flow forever)
\item
  Quantized magnetic flux
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Mechanism (BCS Theory)}\label{mechanism-bcs-theory}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Electrons form Cooper pairs via phonons
\item
  Pairs behave as bosons
\item
  Condense into single quantum state
\item
  No scattering = no resistance
\end{itemize}

\subsection{In Simulation}\label{in-simulation}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\ControlFlowTok{if}\NormalTok{ temperature }\OperatorTok{\textless{}}\NormalTok{ T\_critical:}
\NormalTok{    pair\_electrons(nearby\_electrons)}
    \ControlFlowTok{for}\NormalTok{ pair }\KeywordTok{in}\NormalTok{ cooper\_pairs:}
\NormalTok{        pair.resistance }\OperatorTok{=} \DecValTok{0}
\NormalTok{        pair.locked }\OperatorTok{=} \VariableTok{True}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\subsection{Applications}\label{applications}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  MRI machines
\item
  Particle accelerators
\item
  Maglev trains
\item
  Quantum computers
\end{itemize}

\section{Superfluidity}\label{superfluidity}

Zero viscosity below critical temperature.

\subsection{Properties}\label{properties-6}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Flows without friction
\item
  Climbs container walls
\item
  Quantized vortices
\item
  Second sound (temperature waves)
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Helium-4}\label{helium-4}

At 2.17 K, helium-4 becomes superfluid: - Two-fluid model: normal +
superfluid components - Lambda transition (specific heat spike) - Cannot
be stirred (vortices quantized)

\subsection{In Simulation}\label{in-simulation-1}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\ControlFlowTok{if}\NormalTok{ temperature }\OperatorTok{\textless{}}\NormalTok{ T\_lambda }\KeywordTok{and}\NormalTok{ particle.}\BuiltInTok{type} \OperatorTok{==}\NormalTok{ HELIUM\_4:}
\NormalTok{    particle.viscosity }\OperatorTok{=} \DecValTok{0}
\NormalTok{    quantize\_angular\_momentum(particle)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\section{Bose-Einstein Condensate
(BEC)}\label{bose-einstein-condensate-bec}

At near-absolute zero, bosons occupy the same quantum state.

\subsection{Properties}\label{properties-7}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Macroscopic quantum coherence
\item
  Matter waves visible
\item
  Interference patterns
\item
  Extremely low temperature (nK)
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Creation}\label{creation}

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  Laser cool atoms
\item
  Trap magnetically
\item
  Evaporative cooling
\item
  BEC forms below critical T
\end{enumerate}

\subsection{In Simulation}\label{in-simulation-2}

Multiple particles can share the same voxel if: - They are bosons
(integer spin) - Temperature is near zero - They have identical quantum
states

\section{Quark-Gluon Plasma}\label{quark-gluon-plasma}

At extreme temperature/density, hadrons dissolve.

\subsection{Properties}\label{properties-8}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Quarks and gluons deconfined
\item
  Nearly perfect fluid (lowest viscosity)
\item
  Existed microseconds after Big Bang
\item
  Created briefly in particle colliders
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Conditions}\label{conditions}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Temperature \textgreater{} 2 × 10¹² K
\item
  Energy density \textgreater{} 1 GeV/fm³
\end{itemize}

\subsection{In Simulation}\label{in-simulation-3}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\ControlFlowTok{if}\NormalTok{ temperature }\OperatorTok{\textgreater{}}\NormalTok{ DECONFINEMENT\_TEMP:}
    \ControlFlowTok{for}\NormalTok{ hadron }\KeywordTok{in}\NormalTok{ hadrons:}
\NormalTok{        unlock\_triads(hadron)}
\NormalTok{        release\_quarks(hadron)}
    \CommentTok{\# Free quarks and gluons interact directly}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\section{Neutron Star Matter}\label{neutron-star-matter}

At nuclear densities, matter is mostly neutrons.

\subsection{Layers}\label{layers}

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Outer crust}: Iron lattice + electrons
\item
  \textbf{Inner crust}: Neutron-rich nuclei + neutron drip
\item
  \textbf{Outer core}: Superfluid neutrons + superconducting protons
\item
  \textbf{Inner core}: Unknown: quark matter? strange matter?
\end{enumerate}

\subsection{Properties}\label{properties-9}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Density: 10¹⁴ g/cm³
\item
  Supported by neutron degeneracy pressure
\item
  Intense magnetic fields (10¹² G)
\item
  Millisecond rotation possible
\end{itemize}

\section{Degenerate Matter}\label{degenerate-matter}

When quantum pressure prevents further compression.

\subsection{Electron Degeneracy}\label{electron-degeneracy}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Supports white dwarfs
\item
  Electrons fill lowest states
\item
  Pauli exclusion prevents collapse
\item
  Limit: 1.4 \(M_\odot\) (Chandrasekhar)
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Neutron Degeneracy}\label{neutron-degeneracy}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Supports neutron stars
\item
  Neutrons fill lowest states
\item
  Limit: \textasciitilde3 \(M_\odot\) (uncertain)
\end{itemize}

\section{Strange Matter}\label{strange-matter}

Hypothetical stable matter containing strange quarks.

\subsection{Properties (Theoretical)}\label{properties-theoretical}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Lower energy than nuclear matter (?)
\item
  Self-stabilizing
\item
  Would convert normal matter on contact
\item
  May exist in neutron star cores
\end{itemize}

\section{Experiments}\label{experiments-10}

\subsection{Superconductor Simulation}\label{superconductor-simulation}

Cool material below critical temperature. Watch resistance vanish.

\subsection{Superfluid Demo}\label{superfluid-demo}

Cool helium. Watch it climb walls and flow without friction.
