Cool bosons toward absolute zero. Watch them condense.

\section{Concepts}\label{concepts-10}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{superconductivity}: Zero electrical resistance
\item
  \textbf{superfluidity}: Zero viscosity
\item
  \textbf{bose-einstein-condensate}: Bosons in same quantum state
\item
  \textbf{quark-gluon-plasma}: Deconfined quarks and gluons
\end{itemize}

\section{Transition}\label{transition-10}

Exotic states show matter's quantum nature at large scales. Now we
explore how matter organizes: from crystals to semiconductors to
biological structures.

\part{Structures and Materials}

\chapter{Crystal Lattices}\label{sec-crystal-lattices}

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, leftrule=.75mm, title=\textcolor{quarto-callout-warning-color}{\faExclamationTriangle}\hspace{0.5em}{Pedagogical Context {[}NOT DERIVED FROM FTD{]}}, bottomrule=.15mm, opacityback=0, coltitle=black, arc=.35mm, colframe=quarto-callout-warning-color-frame, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-warning-color!10!white, colback=white, titlerule=0mm, breakable, bottomtitle=1mm, toptitle=1mm, rightrule=.15mm, toprule=.15mm, left=2mm, opacitybacktitle=0.6]

This chapter provides \textbf{standard crystallography context}. While
FTD proposes a mechanism for crystal structure (see callout below),
\textbf{no quantitative predictions} (lattice constants, crystal
energies) are derived from FTD axioms at this scale. The mechanisms
described are {[}CONJECTURE{]}.

\end{tcolorbox}

\emph{``Order in the solid state''}

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, leftrule=.75mm, title=\textcolor{quarto-callout-note-color}{\faInfo}\hspace{0.5em}{Key Revelation}, bottomrule=.15mm, opacityback=0, coltitle=black, arc=.35mm, colframe=quarto-callout-note-color-frame, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-note-color!10!white, colback=white, titlerule=0mm, breakable, bottomtitle=1mm, toptitle=1mm, rightrule=.15mm, toprule=.15mm, left=2mm, opacitybacktitle=0.6]

Crystals are not random: they follow strict geometric rules. Only 14
fundamental lattice types exist in 3D, and all crystalline matter fits
into these patterns.

\end{tcolorbox}

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, leftrule=.75mm, title=\textcolor{quarto-callout-note-color}{\faInfo}\hspace{0.5em}{How FTD Proposes Crystal Structure {[}CONJECTURE{]}}, bottomrule=.15mm, opacityback=0, coltitle=black, arc=.35mm, colframe=quarto-callout-note-color-frame, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-note-color!10!white, colback=white, titlerule=0mm, breakable, bottomtitle=1mm, toptitle=1mm, rightrule=.15mm, toprule=.15mm, left=2mm, opacitybacktitle=0.6]

The 14 Bravais lattices are \emph{proposed} to emerge from FTD's
discrete substrate:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{The simulation lattice is cubic}, providing the natural
  coordinate system
\item
  \textbf{Atomic positions} are minima of the flux potential
\item
  \textbf{Lattice constants} emerge from equilibrium between attractive
  (strong/EM) and repulsive (Pauli) flux gradients
\item
  \textbf{Crystal symmetries} reflect the point group symmetries of the
  flux field
\end{itemize}

The discrete nature of FTD space naturally admits discrete crystal
structures.

\end{tcolorbox}

\section{Bravais Lattices}\label{bravais-lattices}

There are exactly 14 ways to fill 3D space with a repeating pattern.

\subsection{The Seven Crystal Systems}\label{the-seven-crystal-systems}

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 8\tabcolsep) * \real{0.1818}}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 8\tabcolsep) * \real{0.2273}}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 8\tabcolsep) * \real{0.1818}}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 8\tabcolsep) * \real{0.2045}}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 8\tabcolsep) * \real{0.2045}}@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
\begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
System
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
Lattices
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
Angles
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
Lengths
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
Example
\end{minipage} \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
Cubic & 3 & 90°, 90°, 90° & a = b = c & NaCl, Fe, Cu \\
Tetragonal & 2 & 90°, 90°, 90° & a = b \(\neq\) c & Tin,
\(\text{TiO}_2\) \\
Orthorhombic & 4 & 90°, 90°, 90° & a \(\neq\) b \(\neq\) c & Sulfur \\
Hexagonal & 1 & 90°, 90°, 120° & a = b \(\neq\) c & Graphite, Zn \\
Trigonal & 1 & \(\alpha = \beta = \gamma \neq 90°\) & a = b = c &
Quartz \\
Monoclinic & 2 & 90°, \(\beta\), 90° & a \(\neq\) b \(\neq\) c &
Gypsum \\
Triclinic & 1 & \(\alpha \neq \beta \neq \gamma\) & a \(\neq\) b
\(\neq\) c & Most organics \\
\end{longtable}

\section{Cubic Lattices}\label{cubic-lattices}

The most common metallic structures.

\subsection{Simple Cubic (SC)}\label{simple-cubic-sc}

\begin{figure}

\centering{

\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{chapters/../../media/images/fig-simple-cubic.png}}

}

\caption{\label{fig-simple-cubic}Simple cubic lattice: atoms at corners
only, coordination number 6, packing efficiency 52\%.}

\end{figure}%

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Coordination}: 6 nearest neighbors
\item
  \textbf{Packing efficiency}: 52\%
\item
  \textbf{Example}: Polonium
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Body-Centered Cubic (BCC)}\label{body-centered-cubic-bcc}

\begin{figure}

\centering{

\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{chapters/../../media/images/fig-bcc.png}}

}

\caption{\label{fig-bcc}Body-centered cubic lattice: atoms at corners
plus one atom in the center of the cube.}

\end{figure}%

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Coordination}: 8 nearest neighbors
\item
  \textbf{Packing efficiency}: 68\%
\item
  \textbf{Examples}: Fe, W, Cr, Na
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Face-Centered Cubic (FCC)}\label{face-centered-cubic-fcc}

\begin{figure}

\centering{

\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{chapters/../../media/images/fig-fcc.png}}

}

\caption{\label{fig-fcc}Face-centered cubic lattice: atoms at corners
plus atoms at the center of each face.}

\end{figure}%

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Coordination}: 12 nearest neighbors
\item
  \textbf{Packing efficiency}: 74\%
\item
  \textbf{Examples}: Cu, Al, Au, Ag
\end{itemize}

\section{Hexagonal Close-Packed (HCP)}\label{hexagonal-close-packed-hcp}

Alternative to FCC with same packing efficiency.

\begin{figure}

\centering{

\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{chapters/../../media/images/fig-hcp.png}}

}

\caption{\label{fig-hcp}Hexagonal close-packed lattice: alternating
layers in ABABAB stacking pattern.}

\end{figure}%

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Stacking sequence}: ABABAB\ldots{} (2 layers repeat)
\item
  \textbf{Coordination}: 12 nearest neighbors
\item
  \textbf{Packing efficiency}: 74\%
\item
  \textbf{Examples}: Mg, Zn, Ti
\end{itemize}

\subsection{FCC vs HCP}\label{fcc-vs-hcp}

Both are close-packed (74\%) but different stacking: - FCC:
ABCABC\ldots{} (3 layers repeat) - HCP: ABABAB\ldots{} (2 layers repeat)

\section{Diamond Cubic}\label{diamond-cubic}

\begin{figure}

\centering{

\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{chapters/../../media/images/fig-diamond-cubic.png}}

}

\caption{\label{fig-diamond-cubic}Diamond cubic lattice: each atom
tetrahedrally bonded to four neighbors.}

\end{figure}%

Each atom bonds to four others in a tetrahedral arrangement, creating an
open structure with remarkable properties:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Coordination}: 4 nearest neighbors
\item
  \textbf{Packing efficiency}: 34\% (very open structure)
\item
  \textbf{Examples}: C (diamond), Si, Ge
\end{itemize}

\section{Defects}\label{defects}

Real crystals have imperfections.

\subsection{Point Defects}\label{point-defects}

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}lll@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
Type & Description & Effect \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
Vacancy & Missing atom & Diffusion \\
Interstitial & Extra atom & Strengthening \\
Substitutional & Wrong atom & Alloying \\
\end{longtable}

\subsection{Line Defects
(Dislocations)}\label{line-defects-dislocations}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Edge dislocation}: Extra half-plane of atoms
\item
  \textbf{Screw dislocation}: Helical distortion
\end{itemize}

Dislocations allow plastic deformation.

\subsection{Planar Defects}\label{planar-defects}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Grain boundaries}: Between crystal regions
\item
  \textbf{Stacking faults}: ABCABABC instead of ABCABC
\item
  \textbf{Twin boundaries}: Mirror-image regions
\end{itemize}

\section{Miller Indices}\label{miller-indices}

Notation for crystal planes and directions.

\subsection{Planes}\label{planes}

\[(hkl)\]

Example: (100) is perpendicular to x-axis.

\subsection{Directions}\label{directions}

\[[uvw]\]

Example: {[}111{]} is body diagonal.

\section{X-Ray Diffraction}\label{x-ray-diffraction}

How we determine crystal structures.

\subsection{Bragg's Law}\label{braggs-law}

\[
n\lambda = 2d\sin\theta
\]

X-rays reflect off crystal planes. Constructive interference reveals
plane spacing.

\section{Experiments}\label{experiments-11}

\subsection{Crystal Builder}\label{crystal-builder}

Stack atoms in different arrangements. See Bravais lattices form.

\subsection{Defect Simulator}\label{defect-simulator}

Introduce vacancies, interstitials. Watch how properties change.

\subsection{Diffraction Pattern}\label{diffraction-pattern}

Shine X-rays on crystal. See diffraction spots. Deduce structure.

\section{Concepts}\label{concepts-11}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{bravais-lattice}: 14 fundamental 3D lattice types
\item
  \textbf{coordination-number}: Number of nearest neighbors
\item
  \textbf{packing-efficiency}: Fraction of space filled
\item
  \textbf{crystal-defect}: Imperfection in lattice
\end{itemize}

\section{Transition}\label{transition-11}

Crystals provide structure. Now we explore how electrons move through
materials: metals, insulators, and everything between.

\chapter{Metals and Conductors}\label{sec-metals-conductors}

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, leftrule=.75mm, title=\textcolor{quarto-callout-warning-color}{\faExclamationTriangle}\hspace{0.5em}{Pedagogical Context {[}NOT DERIVED FROM FTD{]}}, bottomrule=.15mm, opacityback=0, coltitle=black, arc=.35mm, colframe=quarto-callout-warning-color-frame, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-warning-color!10!white, colback=white, titlerule=0mm, breakable, bottomtitle=1mm, toptitle=1mm, rightrule=.15mm, toprule=.15mm, left=2mm, opacitybacktitle=0.6]

This chapter provides \textbf{standard solid-state physics context}.
While FTD proposes a mechanism for metallic conduction (see callout
below), \textbf{no quantitative predictions} (conductivities, band
structures) are derived from FTD axioms at this scale. The mechanisms
described are {[}CONJECTURE{]}.

\end{tcolorbox}

\emph{``The electron sea''}

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, leftrule=.75mm, title=\textcolor{quarto-callout-note-color}{\faInfo}\hspace{0.5em}{Key Revelation}, bottomrule=.15mm, opacityback=0, coltitle=black, arc=.35mm, colframe=quarto-callout-note-color-frame, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-note-color!10!white, colback=white, titlerule=0mm, breakable, bottomtitle=1mm, toptitle=1mm, rightrule=.15mm, toprule=.15mm, left=2mm, opacitybacktitle=0.6]

Metals conduct because their electrons are delocalized: not bound to
individual atoms but free to flow throughout the material. The electron
sea explains all metallic properties.

\end{tcolorbox}

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, leftrule=.75mm, title=\textcolor{quarto-callout-note-color}{\faInfo}\hspace{0.5em}{How FTD Proposes Metallic Conduction {[}CONJECTURE{]}}, bottomrule=.15mm, opacityback=0, coltitle=black, arc=.35mm, colframe=quarto-callout-note-color-frame, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-note-color!10!white, colback=white, titlerule=0mm, breakable, bottomtitle=1mm, toptitle=1mm, rightrule=.15mm, toprule=.15mm, left=2mm, opacitybacktitle=0.6]

The ``electron sea'' is \emph{proposed} to arise from FTD's flux
structure:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Delocalized electrons} = flux distributions spanning the
  entire lattice rather than localized around nuclei
\item
  \textbf{Conductivity} = flux can propagate freely through the extended
  electron wavefunction
\item
  \textbf{Resistance} = flux scattering from lattice vibrations
  (phonons) and impurities
\item
  \textbf{Fermi surface} = boundary in flux-momentum space between
  occupied and unoccupied states
\end{itemize}

Band theory emerges from the periodic boundary conditions of flux in a
crystalline lattice.

\end{tcolorbox}

\section{The Electron Sea Model}\label{the-electron-sea-model}

In metals, valence electrons are shared among all atoms.

\begin{figure}

\centering{

\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{chapters/../../media/images/fig-electron-sea.png}}

}

\caption{\label{fig-electron-sea}The electron sea model: metal cations
(fixed positive ions) surrounded by a sea of delocalized mobile
electrons.}

\end{figure}%

The metal cations remain fixed in the lattice while electrons move
freely throughout the material, explaining metallic bonding and
conductivity.

\section{Properties Explained}\label{properties-explained}

\subsection{Electrical Conductivity}\label{electrical-conductivity}

Electrons move freely in response to electric field: \[
\mathbf{J} = \sigma \mathbf{E}
\]

Current flows because electrons can move without jumping between atoms.

\subsection{Thermal Conductivity}\label{thermal-conductivity}

Electrons carry heat: \[
\kappa = \frac{1}{3} C_v v \lambda
\]

Metals conduct heat well because electrons move fast and far.

\subsection{Malleability and
Ductility}\label{malleability-and-ductility}

Layers of ions can slide past each other without breaking bonds: the
electron sea reforms around the new positions. When force is applied,
atomic planes can shift while maintaining metallic bonding, unlike ionic
or covalent solids that would shatter.

\subsection{Metallic Luster}\label{metallic-luster}

Free electrons oscillate in response to light and re-emit it: metals are
shiny.

\subsection{Opacity}\label{opacity}

Electrons absorb photons across a continuous range: metals are opaque.

\section{Band Theory}\label{band-theory}

A more sophisticated view of electron behavior.

\subsection{Energy Bands}\label{energy-bands}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Valence band}: Filled electron states
\item
  \textbf{Conduction band}: Empty or partially filled states
\item
  \textbf{Band gap}: Energy difference between bands
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Metal Band Structure}\label{metal-band-structure}

\begin{figure}

\centering{

\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{chapters/../../media/images/fig-metal-band-structure.png}}

}

\caption{\label{fig-metal-band-structure}Metal band structure:
overlapping valence and conduction bands with no energy gap, allowing
free electron movement.}

\end{figure}%

In metals, the valence and conduction bands overlap or touch, with the
conduction band being partially filled. There is no energy gap, so
electrons can move freely in response to applied fields.

\section{Resistance}\label{resistance}

Even metals have some resistance.

\subsection{Sources}\label{sources}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Phonon scattering}: Vibrating atoms deflect electrons
\item
  \textbf{Impurity scattering}: Foreign atoms deflect electrons
\item
  \textbf{Defect scattering}: Lattice imperfections deflect electrons
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Temperature Dependence}\label{temperature-dependence}

\[
\rho(T) = \rho_0 (1 + \alpha T)
\]

Resistance increases with temperature (more phonons).

\section{Conductivity Values}\label{conductivity-values}

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}lll@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
Material & Conductivity (S/m) & Notes \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
Silver & \(6.3 \times 10^7\) & Best conductor \\
Copper & \(5.9 \times 10^7\) & Most common wire \\
Gold & \(4.5 \times 10^7\) & Corrosion resistant \\
Aluminum & \(3.8 \times 10^7\) & Lightweight \\
Iron & \(1.0 \times 10^7\) & Magnetic \\
Graphite & \(10^5\) & Layered conductor \\
Glass & \(10^{-12}\) & Insulator \\
\end{longtable}

\section{Free Electron Model}\label{free-electron-model}

Treating electrons as non-interacting particles.

\subsection{Fermi Energy}\label{fermi-energy}

Maximum electron energy at absolute zero: \[
E_F = \frac{\hbar^2}{2m}\left(\frac{3\pi^2 n}{V}\right)^{2/3}
\]

\subsection{Fermi Velocity}\label{fermi-velocity}

\[
v_F = \sqrt{\frac{2E_F}{m}} \approx 10^6 \text{ m/s}
\]

Electrons at the Fermi surface move very fast.

\subsection{Fermi-Dirac Distribution}\label{fermi-dirac-distribution}

Probability of state being occupied: \[
f(E) = \frac{1}{e^{(E-E_F)/k_BT} + 1}
\]

At T = 0, sharp cutoff at \(E_F\).

\section{In Simulation}\label{in-simulation-4}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\KeywordTok{class}\NormalTok{ MetalRegion:}
    \KeywordTok{def} \FunctionTok{\_\_init\_\_}\NormalTok{(}\VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{, atoms):}
        \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.ions }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ atoms  }\CommentTok{\# Fixed positive ions}
        \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.electrons }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ pool\_electrons(atoms)  }\CommentTok{\# Shared}

    \KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ apply\_field(}\VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{, E\_field):}
        \ControlFlowTok{for}\NormalTok{ electron }\KeywordTok{in} \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.electrons:}
            \CommentTok{\# Electrons respond to field}
\NormalTok{            electron.flux }\OperatorTok{+=}\NormalTok{ ELECTRON\_CHARGE }\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ E\_field}
            \CommentTok{\# Random scattering}
            \ControlFlowTok{if}\NormalTok{ random() }\OperatorTok{\textless{}}\NormalTok{ scattering\_rate:}
\NormalTok{                randomize\_direction(electron.flux)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\section{Experiments}\label{experiments-12}

\subsection{Conductivity Measurement}\label{conductivity-measurement}

Apply voltage. Measure current. Calculate resistance.

\subsection{Temperature Dependence}\label{temperature-dependence-1}

Heat a wire. Watch resistance increase.

\subsection{Electron Flow
Visualization}\label{electron-flow-visualization}

See electrons respond to applied field. Watch current flow.

\section{Concepts}\label{concepts-12}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{electron-sea}: Delocalized valence electrons
\item
  \textbf{band-structure}: Allowed and forbidden energy ranges
\item
  \textbf{fermi-energy}: Maximum electron energy at T=0
\item
  \textbf{conductivity}: Ability to carry current
\end{itemize}

\section{Transition}\label{transition-12}

Metals conduct freely. Insulators do not conduct at all. Between them
are semiconductors: the basis of modern electronics.

\chapter{Semiconductors}\label{sec-semiconductors}

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, leftrule=.75mm, title=\textcolor{quarto-callout-warning-color}{\faExclamationTriangle}\hspace{0.5em}{Pedagogical Context {[}NOT DERIVED FROM FTD{]}}, bottomrule=.15mm, opacityback=0, coltitle=black, arc=.35mm, colframe=quarto-callout-warning-color-frame, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-warning-color!10!white, colback=white, titlerule=0mm, breakable, bottomtitle=1mm, toptitle=1mm, rightrule=.15mm, toprule=.15mm, left=2mm, opacitybacktitle=0.6]

This chapter provides \textbf{standard semiconductor physics context}.
\textbf{No quantitative predictions} (band gaps, doping effects) are
derived from FTD axioms at this scale.

\end{tcolorbox}

\emph{``The foundation of computing''}

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, leftrule=.75mm, title=\textcolor{quarto-callout-note-color}{\faInfo}\hspace{0.5em}{Key Revelation}, bottomrule=.15mm, opacityback=0, coltitle=black, arc=.35mm, colframe=quarto-callout-note-color-frame, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-note-color!10!white, colback=white, titlerule=0mm, breakable, bottomtitle=1mm, toptitle=1mm, rightrule=.15mm, toprule=.15mm, left=2mm, opacitybacktitle=0.6]

Semiconductors can be switched between conducting and insulating. This
controllability enables transistors, and transistors enable computers.

\end{tcolorbox}

\section{Between Metal and Insulator}\label{between-metal-and-insulator}

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}lll@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
Material & Band Gap & Conductivity \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
Metal & 0 & High \\
Semiconductor & 0.1-3 eV & Moderate \\
Insulator & \textgreater3 eV & Very low \\
\end{longtable}

\section{Intrinsic Semiconductors}\label{intrinsic-semiconductors}

Pure silicon or germanium.

\subsection{Band Structure}\label{band-structure}

\begin{figure}

\centering{

\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{chapters/../../media/images/fig-band-structure.png}}

}

\caption{\label{fig-band-structure}Semiconductor band structure: a
moderate energy gap (1.1 eV for silicon) separates the filled valence
band from the empty conduction band.}

\end{figure}%

At absolute zero, the valence band is completely filled and the
conduction band is empty. The band gap (1.1 eV for silicon) determines
the semiconductor's properties.

\subsection{Thermal Excitation}\label{thermal-excitation}

At T \textgreater{} 0, some electrons gain enough energy to jump the
gap: \[
n = n_0 \exp\left(-\frac{E_g}{2k_BT}\right)
\]

\subsection{Carriers}\label{carriers}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Electrons}: Negative carriers in conduction band
\item
  \textbf{Holes}: Positive carriers in valence band (missing electrons)
\end{itemize}

Both contribute to conductivity.

\section{Doping}\label{doping}

Adding impurities to control carrier concentration.

\subsection{n-Type (Add Group V: P, As,
Sb)}\label{n-type-add-group-v-p-as-sb}

\begin{figure}

\centering{

\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{chapters/../../media/images/fig-n-type-doping.png}}

}

\caption{\label{fig-n-type-doping}n-type doping: a Group V atom (like
phosphorus) in a silicon lattice provides one extra electron.}

\end{figure}%

Phosphorus has 5 valence electrons. Four bond with silicon neighbors,
leaving one extra electron free to conduct.

\subsection{p-Type (Add Group III: B, Ga,
Al)}\label{p-type-add-group-iii-b-ga-al}

\begin{figure}

\centering{

\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{chapters/../../media/images/fig-p-type-doping.png}}

}

\caption{\label{fig-p-type-doping}p-type doping: a Group III atom (like
boron) in a silicon lattice creates one hole (missing electron).}

\end{figure}%

Boron has only 3 valence electrons. It bonds with four silicon
neighbors, leaving one hole that can accept an electron.

\subsection{Carrier Concentrations}\label{carrier-concentrations}

For n-type: \(n >> p\), electrons dominate For p-type: \(p >> n\), holes
dominate

\section{The p-n Junction}\label{the-p-n-junction}

Interface between p-type and n-type regions.

\subsection{Formation}\label{formation-1}

\begin{figure}

\centering{

\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{chapters/../../media/images/fig-pn-junction.png}}

}

\caption{\label{fig-pn-junction}The p-n junction: when p-type and n-type
semiconductors meet, electrons and holes recombine at the interface,
creating a depletion zone.}

\end{figure}%

At the junction, electrons from the n-side diffuse into the p-side and
recombine with holes. This creates a \textbf{depletion zone}: a region
with no free carriers, only fixed charged ions (negative on p-side,
positive on n-side).

\subsection{Depletion Zone}\label{depletion-zone}

Region with no free carriers. Acts as insulator.

\subsection{Built-in Voltage}\label{built-in-voltage}

Electric field from exposed ions creates potential barrier.

\section{Diode Behavior}\label{diode-behavior}

\subsection{Forward Bias (+ on p-side)}\label{forward-bias-on-p-side}

Shrinks depletion zone. Current flows.

\subsection{Reverse Bias (+ on n-side)}\label{reverse-bias-on-n-side}

Expands depletion zone. No current (blocking).

\subsection{I-V Characteristic}\label{i-v-characteristic}

\[
I = I_0 \left(e^{qV/k_BT} - 1\right)
\]

Exponential in forward direction, blocked in reverse.

\section{Transistor}\label{transistor}

The switch that enables computing.

\subsection{Basic Types}\label{basic-types}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{BJT} (Bipolar Junction Transistor): npn or pnp
\item
  \textbf{MOSFET} (Metal-Oxide-Semiconductor FET): Most common today
\end{itemize}

\subsection{MOSFET Structure}\label{mosfet-structure}

\begin{figure}

\centering{

\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{chapters/../../media/images/fig-mosfet.png}}

}

\caption{\label{fig-mosfet}MOSFET structure: gate voltage controls the
conductivity of the channel between source and drain.}

\end{figure}%

The Metal-Oxide-Semiconductor Field-Effect Transistor has three
terminals: source, drain, and gate. Applying voltage to the gate creates
(or removes) a conducting channel between source and drain.

\subsection{Switching}\label{switching}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Gate voltage low: Channel off (insulating)
\item
  Gate voltage high: Channel on (conducting)
\end{itemize}

This is how 0s and 1s are represented.

\section{In Simulation}\label{in-simulation-5}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\KeywordTok{class}\NormalTok{ Semiconductor:}
    \KeywordTok{def} \FunctionTok{\_\_init\_\_}\NormalTok{(}\VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{, base\_material, dopant\_type, dopant\_concentration):}
        \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.material }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ base\_material}
        \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.}\BuiltInTok{type} \OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ dopant\_type}
        \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.carrier\_density }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ calculate\_carriers(dopant\_concentration)}

    \KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ conductivity(}\VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{, temperature, electric\_field):}
        \CommentTok{\# Intrinsic carriers}
\NormalTok{        n\_i }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ intrinsic\_carrier(}\VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.material, temperature)}

        \CommentTok{\# Doped carriers}
        \ControlFlowTok{if} \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.}\BuiltInTok{type} \OperatorTok{==}\NormalTok{ N\_TYPE:}
\NormalTok{            n }\OperatorTok{=} \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.carrier\_density}
\NormalTok{            p }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ n\_i}\OperatorTok{**}\DecValTok{2} \OperatorTok{/}\NormalTok{ n}
        \ControlFlowTok{else}\NormalTok{:}
\NormalTok{            p }\OperatorTok{=} \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.carrier\_density}
\NormalTok{            n }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ n\_i}\OperatorTok{**}\DecValTok{2} \OperatorTok{/}\NormalTok{ p}

        \ControlFlowTok{return}\NormalTok{ ELECTRON\_MOBILITY }\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ n }\OperatorTok{+}\NormalTok{ HOLE\_MOBILITY }\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ p}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\section{Experiments}\label{experiments-13}

\subsection{Doping Effect}\label{doping-effect}

Add impurities to pure semiconductor. Watch conductivity change.

\subsection{p-n Junction}\label{p-n-junction}

Build junction. Apply forward and reverse bias. See diode behavior.

\subsection{Transistor Switch}\label{transistor-switch}

Build transistor. Control current with gate voltage.

\section{Concepts}\label{concepts-13}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{band-gap}: Energy between valence and conduction bands
\item
  \textbf{doping}: Adding impurities to control carriers
\item
  \textbf{p-n-junction}: Interface between p-type and n-type
\item
  \textbf{transistor}: Voltage-controlled switch
\end{itemize}

\section{Transition}\label{transition-13}

Semiconductors enable electronics. But life uses different structures:
biological materials with unique properties. Next, we explore the
building blocks of life.

\chapter{Biological Structures}\label{sec-biological-structures}

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, leftrule=.75mm, title=\textcolor{quarto-callout-warning-color}{\faExclamationTriangle}\hspace{0.5em}{Pedagogical Context {[}NOT DERIVED FROM FTD{]}}, bottomrule=.15mm, opacityback=0, coltitle=black, arc=.35mm, colframe=quarto-callout-warning-color-frame, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-warning-color!10!white, colback=white, titlerule=0mm, breakable, bottomtitle=1mm, toptitle=1mm, rightrule=.15mm, toprule=.15mm, left=2mm, opacitybacktitle=0.6]

This chapter provides \textbf{standard biochemistry/biophysics context}.
While FTD proposes a mechanism for biological structure (see callout
below), \textbf{no quantitative predictions} (protein folding energies,
DNA stability) are derived from FTD axioms at this scale. The mechanisms
described are {[}CONJECTURE{]}.

\end{tcolorbox}

\emph{``Life's building blocks''}

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, leftrule=.75mm, title=\textcolor{quarto-callout-note-color}{\faInfo}\hspace{0.5em}{Key Revelation}, bottomrule=.15mm, opacityback=0, coltitle=black, arc=.35mm, colframe=quarto-callout-note-color-frame, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-note-color!10!white, colback=white, titlerule=0mm, breakable, bottomtitle=1mm, toptitle=1mm, rightrule=.15mm, toprule=.15mm, left=2mm, opacitybacktitle=0.6]

Life builds with the same atoms as rocks and metals, but organizes them
into self-replicating, energy-processing, information-storing
structures. The architecture of life is molecular.

\end{tcolorbox}

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, leftrule=.75mm, title=\textcolor{quarto-callout-note-color}{\faInfo}\hspace{0.5em}{How FTD Proposes Biological Structure {[}CONJECTURE{]}}, bottomrule=.15mm, opacityback=0, coltitle=black, arc=.35mm, colframe=quarto-callout-note-color-frame, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-note-color!10!white, colback=white, titlerule=0mm, breakable, bottomtitle=1mm, toptitle=1mm, rightrule=.15mm, toprule=.15mm, left=2mm, opacitybacktitle=0.6]

Biological organization is \emph{proposed} to emerge from the same flux
dynamics governing all matter:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Molecular stability} = flux configurations that minimize local
  energy (hydrogen bonds, van der Waals)
\item
  \textbf{Self-assembly} = entropy-driven flux redistribution toward
  lower free energy states
\item
  \textbf{Information storage} = DNA base pairing as specific
  flux-mediated hydrogen bond patterns
\item
  \textbf{Metabolism} = controlled flux transfer between molecular
  configurations
\end{itemize}

Life is organized matter: flux configurations that maintain and
replicate themselves against entropic dissipation.

\end{tcolorbox}

\section{The Cell Membrane}\label{the-cell-membrane}

The boundary between life and non-life.

\subsection{Lipid Bilayer}\label{lipid-bilayer}

\begin{figure}

\centering{

\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{chapters/../../media/images/fig-lipid-bilayer.png}}

}

\caption{\label{fig-lipid-bilayer}The lipid bilayer: phospholipids
self-assemble with hydrophilic heads facing water and hydrophobic tails
forming the membrane core.}

\end{figure}%

Phospholipids spontaneously form this structure because their
hydrophilic (water-loving) heads face the aqueous environment while
their hydrophobic (water-fearing) tails cluster together in the membrane
interior.

\subsection{Properties}\label{properties-10}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Selectively permeable
\item
  Fluid (molecules move laterally)
\item
  Self-healing
\item
  5-10 nm thick
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Membrane Proteins}\label{membrane-proteins}

Embedded in the bilayer:

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}ll@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
Type & Function \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
Channels & Allow specific ions through \\
Transporters & Move molecules against gradient \\
Receptors & Detect signals \\
Enzymes & Catalyze reactions \\
\end{longtable}

\section{Cytoskeleton}\label{cytoskeleton}

The internal scaffold of cells.

\subsection{Microfilaments (Actin)}\label{microfilaments-actin}

The thinnest cytoskeletal elements (7 nm diameter), consisting of two
twisted strands of actin protein.

\textbf{Functions}: Cell shape maintenance, cell movement, muscle
contraction

\subsection{Intermediate Filaments}\label{intermediate-filaments}

Medium-sized filaments (10 nm diameter) with a rope-like structure.

\textbf{Functions}: Mechanical strength, nuclear envelope support,
tissue integrity

\subsection{Microtubules}\label{microtubules}

The largest cytoskeletal elements (25 nm diameter), hollow tubes made of
tubulin protein.

\textbf{Functions}: Intracellular transport highways, cell division
(mitotic spindle), cilia and flagella structure

\section{DNA Structure}\label{dna-structure-1}

The information molecule.

\subsection{Double Helix}\label{double-helix-1}

\begin{figure}

\centering{

\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{chapters/../../media/images/fig-dna-helix.png}}

}

\caption{\label{fig-dna-helix}The DNA double helix: two antiparallel
strands connected by base pairs (A-T with 2 hydrogen bonds, G-C with 3
hydrogen bonds).}

\end{figure}%

The structure consists of: - \textbf{Sugar-phosphate backbone}:
Alternating deoxyribose sugars and phosphate groups - \textbf{Base
pairs}: Adenine pairs with Thymine (2 H-bonds), Guanine pairs with
Cytosine (3 H-bonds) - \textbf{Antiparallel strands}: One runs 5'
\(\to\) 3', the other 3' \(\to\) 5'

\subsection{Key Properties}\label{key-properties}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Antiparallel strands (5' \(\to\) 3', 3' \(\to\) 5')
\item
  10.5 base pairs per turn
\item
  Major and minor grooves
\item
  2 nm diameter
\end{itemize}

\section{Protein Structure}\label{protein-structure-1}

\subsection{Levels}\label{levels}

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}lll@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
Level & Description & Stabilized by \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
Primary & Amino acid sequence & Covalent bonds \\
Secondary & \(\alpha\)-helix, \(\beta\)-sheet & Hydrogen bonds \\
Tertiary & 3D fold & All forces \\
Quaternary & Multiple chains & All forces \\
\end{longtable}

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{\(\alpha\)-Helix}{\textbackslash alpha-Helix}}\label{alpha-helix}

\begin{figure}

\centering{

\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{chapters/../../media/images/fig-alpha-helix.png}}

}

\caption{\label{fig-alpha-helix}The alpha-helix: a coiled structure with
3.6 amino acid residues per turn, stabilized by hydrogen bonds parallel
to the helix axis.}

\end{figure}%

The backbone spirals upward with hydrogen bonds connecting every fourth
residue, creating a stable rod-like structure.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{\(\beta\)-Sheet}{\textbackslash beta-Sheet}}\label{beta-sheet}

\begin{figure}

\centering{

\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{chapters/../../media/images/fig-beta-sheet.png}}

}

\caption{\label{fig-beta-sheet}The beta-sheet: extended strands
connected side-by-side by hydrogen bonds, forming a pleated sheet
structure.}

\end{figure}%

Strands can run in the same direction (parallel) or opposite directions
(antiparallel), connected by hydrogen bonds between backbone atoms.

\section{Organelles}\label{organelles}

Compartments within cells.

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}lll@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
Organelle & Function & Key Feature \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
Nucleus & DNA storage & Double membrane \\
Mitochondria & ATP production & Own DNA \\
Ribosome & Protein synthesis & RNA + protein \\
ER & Protein processing & Connected to nucleus \\
Golgi & Packaging & Stacked membranes \\
Lysosome & Digestion & Acidic interior \\
Chloroplast & Photosynthesis & Own DNA (plants) \\
\end{longtable}

\section{Self-Assembly}\label{self-assembly}

Biological structures build themselves.

\subsection{Principles}\label{principles}

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Thermodynamic favorability}: Lower free energy
\item
  \textbf{Specific interactions}: Lock and key
\item
  \textbf{Hierarchical}: Small units → larger structures
\item
  \textbf{Dynamic}: Constantly remodeling
\end{enumerate}

\subsection{Examples}\label{examples}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Lipids \(\to\) membranes
\item
  Amino acids \(\to\) proteins \(\to\) complexes
\item
  Nucleotides \(\to\) DNA \(\to\) chromosomes
\end{itemize}

\section{In Simulation}\label{in-simulation-6}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\KeywordTok{class}\NormalTok{ Membrane:}
    \KeywordTok{def} \FunctionTok{\_\_init\_\_}\NormalTok{(}\VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{, phospholipids):}
        \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.lipids }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ phospholipids}
        \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.proteins }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ []}

    \KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ add\_protein(}\VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{, protein):}
        \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.proteins.append(protein)}

    \KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ permeability(}\VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{, molecule):}
        \ControlFlowTok{if}\NormalTok{ molecule.is\_small }\KeywordTok{and}\NormalTok{ molecule.is\_nonpolar:}
            \ControlFlowTok{return}\NormalTok{ HIGH  }\CommentTok{\# Crosses easily}
        \ControlFlowTok{elif}\NormalTok{ molecule }\KeywordTok{in} \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.channel\_substrates:}
            \ControlFlowTok{return}\NormalTok{ MEDIUM  }\CommentTok{\# Channel{-}mediated}
        \ControlFlowTok{else}\NormalTok{:}
            \ControlFlowTok{return}\NormalTok{ LOW  }\CommentTok{\# Blocked}

\KeywordTok{class}\NormalTok{ Protein:}
    \KeywordTok{def} \FunctionTok{\_\_init\_\_}\NormalTok{(}\VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{, sequence):}
        \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.primary }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ sequence}
        \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.secondary }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ predict\_secondary(sequence)}
        \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.tertiary }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ fold(}\VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.secondary)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\section{Experiments}\label{experiments-14}

\subsection{Membrane Assembly}\label{membrane-assembly}

Add phospholipids to water. Watch vesicles form spontaneously.

\subsection{Protein Folding}\label{protein-folding-1}

Create amino acid chain. Watch it fold into functional shape.

\subsection{Cell Building}\label{cell-building}

Assemble organelles within membrane. Create minimal cell.

\section{Concepts}\label{concepts-14}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{lipid-bilayer}: Self-assembled membrane structure
\item
  \textbf{cytoskeleton}: Internal cell scaffold
\item
  \textbf{protein-folding}: Sequence → structure → function
\item
  \textbf{self-assembly}: Spontaneous organization
\end{itemize}

\section{Transition}\label{transition-14}

We have explored matter at molecular scales. Now we zoom out
dramatically: to planets, where gravity dominates and geology shapes
worlds.

\part{The Planetary Realm}

\chapter{Gravity Wells}\label{sec-gravity-wells}

\emph{Having understood how matter organizes from atoms through
materials, we now scale up to planetary bodies where gravity dominates.}

\emph{``How worlds are born''}

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, leftrule=.75mm, title=\textcolor{quarto-callout-note-color}{\faInfo}\hspace{0.5em}{Key Revelation}, bottomrule=.15mm, opacityback=0, coltitle=black, arc=.35mm, colframe=quarto-callout-note-color-frame, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-note-color!10!white, colback=white, titlerule=0mm, breakable, bottomtitle=1mm, toptitle=1mm, rightrule=.15mm, toprule=.15mm, left=2mm, opacitybacktitle=0.6]

Planets form from the same process that governs all scales: matter
following gravity gradients, accumulating until stable structures
emerge.

\end{tcolorbox}

\section{Planetary Formation}\label{planetary-formation}

\begin{figure}

\centering{

\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{chapters/../../media/images/fig-gravity-well-and-formation.png}}

}

\caption{\label{fig-gravity-formation}Gravity well formation: from dust
disk to differentiated planet through accretion, planetesimal growth,
and gravitational collapse.}

\end{figure}%

\subsection{Stage 1: Accretion}\label{stage-1-accretion}

From a disk of gas and dust around a young star, particles collide and
stick. Electrostatic forces hold small grains together, beginning the
process of growth.

\subsection{Stage 2: Planetesimals}\label{stage-2-planetesimals}

When objects reach \textasciitilde1 km:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Gravity becomes significant
\item
  Runaway growth begins
\item
  Larger objects attract more material
\item
  Planetesimals form in \textasciitilde10,000 years
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Stage 3: Protoplanets}\label{stage-3-protoplanets}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Planetesimals collide and merge
\item
  Mars-sized bodies form
\item
  Oligarchic growth (few dominant)
\item
  Takes \textasciitilde100,000 years
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Stage 4: Final Assembly}\label{stage-4-final-assembly}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Giant impacts reshape worlds
\item
  Moon-forming impact (Earth)
\item
  Orbital clearing
\item
  Takes \textasciitilde100 million years
\end{itemize}

\section{Planetary Types}\label{planetary-types}

\subsection{Terrestrial (Rocky)}\label{terrestrial-rocky}

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}ll@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
Property & Range \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
Mass & 0.1-2 \(M_\oplus\) \\
Radius & 0.5-1.5 \(R_\oplus\) \\
Density & 4-6 g/cm³ \\
Surface & Solid \\
Composition & Iron, silicates \\
\end{longtable}

Examples: Mercury, Venus, Earth, Mars

\subsection{Gas Giants}\label{gas-giants}

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}ll@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
Property & Range \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
Mass & 10-500 \(M_\oplus\) \\
Radius & 3-15 \(R_\oplus\) \\
Density & 0.5-2 g/cm³ \\
Surface & None (gas all the way down?) \\
Composition & H, He \\
\end{longtable}

Examples: Jupiter, Saturn

\section{The Gravity Well}\label{the-gravity-well}

Planets sit in gravitational potential wells. The deeper the well, the
more energy required to escape. This is visualized as a surface where
the planet rests at the lowest point, with the surrounding ``walls''
representing the gravitational potential energy that must be overcome.

Escape velocity: \[
v_{\text{escape}} = \sqrt{\frac{2GM}{r}}
\]

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}ll@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
Body & v\_escape \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
Moon & 2.4 km/s \\
Earth & 11.2 km/s \\
Jupiter & 60 km/s \\
Sun & 618 km/s \\
\end{longtable}

\section{General Relativity: Emergence from
FTD}\label{general-relativity-emergence-from-ftd}

In FTD, gravity is a force arising from density gradients:

\[\mathbf{F}_g = G_{\text{bias}} \cdot \nabla\bar{\rho}(x)\]

But general relativity describes gravity as spacetime curvature. How do
these connect?

\subsection{The Effective Metric}\label{the-effective-metric}

The flux density field ρ(x) creates an \textbf{effective metric}:

\[g_{\mu\nu} = \eta_{\mu\nu} + h_{\mu\nu}(\rho)\]

Where: - η\_μν is the flat Minkowski metric - h\_μν is the perturbation
from flux density

In the weak-field limit:

\[h_{00} = -\frac{2\Phi}{c^2}, \quad h_{ij} = -\frac{2\Phi}{c^2}\delta_{ij}\]

Where Φ is the Newtonian potential, related to flux density by:

\[\nabla^2\Phi = 4\pi G \rho\]

\subsection{Geodesic Motion}\label{geodesic-motion}

Free particles in FTD follow \textbf{flux gradients}. In the GR picture,
this is \textbf{geodesic motion}:

\[\frac{d^2x^\mu}{d\tau^2} + \Gamma^\mu_{\nu\lambda}\frac{dx^\nu}{d\tau}\frac{dx^\lambda}{d\tau} = 0\]

The Christoffel symbols Γ are determined by the metric derivatives:
which come from the flux density profile.

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, leftrule=.75mm, title=\textcolor{quarto-callout-note-color}{\faInfo}\hspace{0.5em}{Gravity as Geometry}, bottomrule=.15mm, opacityback=0, coltitle=black, arc=.35mm, colframe=quarto-callout-note-color-frame, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-note-color!10!white, colback=white, titlerule=0mm, breakable, bottomtitle=1mm, toptitle=1mm, rightrule=.15mm, toprule=.15mm, left=2mm, opacitybacktitle=0.6]

FTD's flux gradients create an effective curved geometry. What looks
like a force is actually motion along the straightest possible path
(geodesic) in curved spacetime.

\end{tcolorbox}

\subsection{Einstein's Equations}\label{einsteins-equations}

The linearized Einstein equations:

\[\Box h_{\mu\nu} = -16\pi G T_{\mu\nu}\]

emerge from the FTD wave equation for the flux field:

\[\partial_t^2 J = c^2 \nabla^2 J + \text{sources}\]

The stress-energy tensor T\_μν corresponds to the flux density and
momentum distributions.

\subsection{Beyond Linearized Gravity}\label{beyond-linearized-gravity}

At higher flux densities (strong gravity), nonlinear effects appear: -
Metric perturbations interact with each other - Geodesic deviation
becomes significant - Full Einstein equations emerge

This is why FTD naturally incorporates: - Gravitational waves (flux
ripples) - Black holes (extreme flux concentrations) - Cosmological
expansion (large-scale flux dynamics)

\subsection{Gravitational Waves in
FTD}\label{gravitational-waves-in-ftd}

When massive objects accelerate, flux patterns propagate outward:

\[h_{+,\times} \propto \frac{1}{r}\frac{d^2Q_{ij}}{dt^2}\]

These are gravitational waves: transverse ripples in the flux field
traveling at speed C.

\KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ planetary\_formation(dust\_cloud):}

        \CommentTok{\# Gravity pulls particles together}
\NormalTok{        apply\_gravity(dust\_cloud)}

        \CommentTok{\# Detect forming clumps}
\NormalTok{        clumps }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ find\_density\_peaks(dust\_cloud)}

        \CommentTok{\# Merge nearby clumps}

\NormalTok{                merge(clump, other\_clump)}

            \ControlFlowTok{if}\NormalTok{ hot\_enough(planet):}
\NormalTok{                differentiate(planet)}
\end{Highlighting}

\section{Transition}\label{transition-15}

Planets need more than solid ground. Next, we explore atmospheres: the
gases that surround worlds and enable life.

\chapter{Atmospheres}\label{sec-atmospheres}

\emph{``The breath of worlds''}

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, leftrule=.75mm, title=\textcolor{quarto-callout-note-color}{\faInfo}\hspace{0.5em}{Key Revelation}, bottomrule=.15mm, opacityback=0, coltitle=black, arc=.35mm, colframe=quarto-callout-note-color-frame, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-note-color!10!white, colback=white, titlerule=0mm, breakable, bottomtitle=1mm, toptitle=1mm, rightrule=.15mm, toprule=.15mm, left=2mm, opacitybacktitle=0.6]

An atmosphere is a battle between gravity (holding gas in) and thermal
energy (allowing escape). The winner determines whether a world can
support life.

\end{tcolorbox}

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, leftrule=.75mm, title=\textcolor{quarto-callout-note-color}{\faInfo}\hspace{0.5em}{How FTD Produces Atmospheric Structure}, bottomrule=.15mm, opacityback=0, coltitle=black, arc=.35mm, colframe=quarto-callout-note-color-frame, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-note-color!10!white, colback=white, titlerule=0mm, breakable, bottomtitle=1mm, toptitle=1mm, rightrule=.15mm, toprule=.15mm, left=2mm, opacitybacktitle=0.6]

Atmospheric layering emerges from flux dynamics in a gravitational
field:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Hydrostatic equilibrium} = balance of gravitational flux
  gradient vs thermal flux pressure
\item
  \textbf{Temperature profile} = determined by radiative flux transport
  and convective flux mixing
\item
  \textbf{Escape velocity} = threshold where kinetic flux exceeds
  gravitational binding flux
\item
  \textbf{Layering} = different absorption/emission properties of flux
  at different altitudes
\end{itemize}

The barometric formula follows from flux conservation in a gravitational
potential.

\end{tcolorbox}

\section{Atmospheric Structure}\label{atmospheric-structure}

\item
  \textbf{Mesosphere}: No heat source
\item
  \textbf{Thermosphere}: UV absorption
\end{itemize}

\section{Atmospheric Escape}\label{atmospheric-escape}

\subsection{Jeans Escape}\label{jeans-escape}

Thermal escape when particles exceed escape velocity:

\[
v_{\text{thermal}} = \sqrt{\frac{2k_BT}{m}}
\]

\[
v_{\text{escape}} = \sqrt{\frac{2GM}{r}}
\]

If \(v_{\text{thermal}} > v_{\text{escape}}/6\), significant loss
occurs.

\subsection{Why Hydrogen Escapes}\label{why-hydrogen-escapes}

Light molecules move faster at same temperature: \[
v \propto \frac{1}{\sqrt{m}}
\]

\(\text{H}_2\) moves 4\(\times\) faster than \(\text{O}_2\) \(\to\)
escapes more easily.

\subsection{Atmospheric Retention}\label{atmospheric-retention}

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}llll@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
Planet & Gravity & Temperature & Atmosphere \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
Moon & Weak & Hot days & None \\
Mars & Weak & Cold & Thin \(\text{CO}_2\) \\
Earth & Medium & Moderate & Thick \(\text{N}_2\)/\(\text{O}_2\) \\
Venus & Medium & Hot & Dense \(\text{CO}_2\) \\
Jupiter & Strong & Cold & Massive \(\text{H}_2\)/He \\
\end{longtable}

\section{The Greenhouse Effect}\label{the-greenhouse-effect}

\begin{figure}

\centering{

\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{chapters/../../media/images/fig-atmospheres-core.png}}

}

\caption{\label{fig-atmospheres}Atmospheric structure and the greenhouse
mechanism: sunlight penetrates to the surface, which emits infrared
radiation that is partially trapped by atmospheric gases.}

\end{figure}%

\subsection{Mechanism}\label{mechanism}

Sunlight passes through the atmosphere and is absorbed by the ground.
The warmed surface emits infrared radiation, which greenhouse gases in
the atmosphere absorb and re-emit in all directions, including back
toward the surface. This traps heat and raises the surface temperature
above what it would be without an atmosphere.

\subsection{Energy Balance}\label{energy-balance}

Without atmosphere: \[
T_{\text{eff}} = \left(\frac{(1-A)S}{4\sigma}\right)^{1/4}
\]

With greenhouse gases: \[
T_{\text{surface}} > T_{\text{eff}}
\]

\subsection{Planetary Comparison}\label{planetary-comparison}

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}llll@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
Planet & T\_eff & T\_surface & Δ \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
Venus & 232 K & 737 K & +505 K \\
Earth & 255 K & 288 K & +33 K \\
Mars & 210 K & 210 K & 0 K \\
\end{longtable}

Venus's runaway greenhouse makes it hotter than Mercury.

\section{Weather}\label{weather}

Driven by uneven heating:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist

  \textbf{Hadley cells}: Tropical convection
\item
  \textbf{Trade winds}: Surface return flow
\item
  \textbf{Jet streams}: Upper atmosphere currents
\item
  \textbf{Storms}: Energy redistribution
\end{itemize}

deflection - Southern hemisphere: Leftward deflection

Creates spiral storm patterns.

\section{In Simulation}\label{in-simulation-8}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\KeywordTok{class}\NormalTok{ Atmosphere:}
    \KeywordTok{def} \FunctionTok{\_\_init\_\_}\NormalTok{(}\VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{, planet, composition, mass):}
        \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.planet }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ planet}
        \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.gases }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ composition}
        \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.mass }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ mass}
        \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.scale\_height }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ calculate\_scale\_height(planet, composition)}

    \KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ escape\_rate(}\VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{, species):}
\NormalTok{        v\_thermal }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ sqrt(}\DecValTok{2} \OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ k\_B }\OperatorTok{*} \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.temperature }\OperatorTok{/}\NormalTok{ species.mass)}
\NormalTok{        v\_escape }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ sqrt(}\DecValTok{2} \OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ G }\OperatorTok{*} \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.planet.mass }\OperatorTok{/} \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.planet.radius)}
        \ControlFlowTok{return}\NormalTok{ jeans\_escape\_rate(v\_thermal, v\_escape)}

    \KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ greenhouse\_temperature(}\VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{):}
\NormalTok{        T\_eff }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ effective\_temperature(}\VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.planet)}
\NormalTok{        greenhouse\_factor }\OperatorTok{=} \BuiltInTok{sum}\NormalTok{(gas.absorption }\ControlFlowTok{for}\NormalTok{ gas }\KeywordTok{in} \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.gases)}
        \ControlFlowTok{return}\NormalTok{ T\_eff }\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ (}\DecValTok{1} \OperatorTok{+}\NormalTok{ greenhouse\_factor)}\OperatorTok{**}\FloatTok{0.25}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\chapter{Geology}\label{sec-geology}

\emph{``The solid foundation of worlds''}

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, leftrule=.75mm, title=\textcolor{quarto-callout-note-color}{\faInfo}\hspace{0.5em}{Key Revelation}, bottomrule=.15mm, opacityback=0, coltitle=black, arc=.35mm, colframe=quarto-callout-note-color-frame, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-note-color!10!white, colback=white, titlerule=0mm, breakable, bottomtitle=1mm, toptitle=1mm, rightrule=.15mm, toprule=.15mm, left=2mm, opacitybacktitle=0.6]

Planets are not static: their interiors churn, their surfaces crack, and
their mountains rise over millions of years. Geology is planetary
dynamics in slow motion.

\end{tcolorbox}

\section{Planetary Interiors}\label{planetary-interiors}

\subsection{Structure}\label{structure}

\begin{figure}

\centering{

\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{chapters/../../media/images/fig-geology-core.png}}

}

\caption{\label{fig-geology-structure}Planetary interior structure: from
light silicate crust through dense mantle to metallic core, showing
differentiation by density.}

\end{figure}%

Planets differentiate into layers based on density. The lightest
materials (silicates) rise to form the crust, while progressively denser
materials sink: the mantle of dense silicates, the liquid outer core of
iron-nickel, and the solid inner core at the center.

Earth's interior is still hot from formation + radioactive heating.

\section{Plate Tectonics}\label{plate-tectonics}

\subsection{Driving Forces}\label{driving-forces}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Mantle convection}: Hot material rises, cold sinks
\item
  \textbf{Ridge push}: New crust pushes plates apart
\item
  \textbf{Slab pull}: Dense subducting plates pull
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Plate Boundaries}\label{plate-boundaries}

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}lll@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
Type & Motion & Features \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
Divergent & Apart & Mid-ocean ridges \\
Convergent & Together & Trenches, mountains \\
Transform & Sliding & Earthquakes \\
\end{longtable}

\subsection{The Rock Cycle}\label{the-rock-cycle}

The rock cycle describes the continuous transformation of rocks through
three main types: igneous rocks form from cooled magma, which weather
into sediments that become sedimentary rocks through burial and
compaction, which transform into metamorphic rocks under heat and
pressure, which can melt to form new igneous rocks. This cycle has
operated for billions of years, recycling Earth's crust.

\section{In Simulation}\label{in-simulation-9}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\KeywordTok{class}\NormalTok{ Planet:}
    \KeywordTok{def} \FunctionTok{\_\_init\_\_}\NormalTok{(}\VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{, mass, composition):}
        \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.layers }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ differentiate(mass, composition)}
        \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.plates }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ []}
        \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.heat }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ initial\_heat(mass)}

    \KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ evolve(}\VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{, dt):}
        \CommentTok{\# Convection}
        \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.mantle\_flow }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ convection(}\VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.layers[}\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}mantle\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{])}

        \CommentTok{\# Plate motion}
        \ControlFlowTok{for}\NormalTok{ plate }\KeywordTok{in} \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.plates:}
\NormalTok{            plate.move(}\VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.mantle\_flow)}

        \CommentTok{\# Volcanism}
        \ControlFlowTok{if}\NormalTok{ pressure\_release(}\VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.layers):}
\NormalTok{            create\_volcano()}

        \CommentTok{\# Heat loss}
        \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.heat }\OperatorTok{{-}=}\NormalTok{ surface\_radiation(dt)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\section{Transition}\label{transition-17}

Solid ground and gaseous atmosphere need protection. Next, we explore
magnetospheres: the invisible shields that protect worlds from space.

\chapter{Magnetospheres}\label{sec-magnetospheres}

\emph{``Invisible shields''}

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, leftrule=.75mm, title=\textcolor{quarto-callout-note-color}{\faInfo}\hspace{0.5em}{Key Revelation}, bottomrule=.15mm, opacityback=0, coltitle=black, arc=.35mm, colframe=quarto-callout-note-color-frame, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-note-color!10!white, colback=white, titlerule=0mm, breakable, bottomtitle=1mm, toptitle=1mm, rightrule=.15mm, toprule=.15mm, left=2mm, opacitybacktitle=0.6]

A planet's magnetic field is its shield against the solar wind. Without
one, atmospheres erode and surfaces are irradiated. Earth's
magnetosphere makes life possible.

\end{tcolorbox}

\section{Planetary Magnetic Fields}\label{planetary-magnetic-fields}

\subsection{The Dynamo Mechanism}\label{the-dynamo-mechanism}

Magnetic fields arise from: 1. \textbf{Conducting fluid} (liquid metal
core) 2. \textbf{Convection} (heat-driven motion) 3. \textbf{Rotation}
(Coriolis effect organizes flow)

\[
\frac{\partial \mathbf{B}}{\partial t} = \nabla \times (\mathbf{v} \times \mathbf{B}) + \eta \nabla^2 \mathbf{B}
\]

\subsection{Magnetic Field Comparison}\label{magnetic-field-comparison}

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}lll@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
Planet & Field Strength & Dynamo Active? \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
Mercury & 1\% Earth & Weak \\
Venus & None & No (slow rotation) \\
Earth & 25-65 \(\mu\)T & Yes \\
Mars & Crustal only & Extinct \\
Jupiter & 20× Earth & Very active \\
Saturn & 0.5× Earth & Active \\
\end{longtable}

\section{Magnetosphere Structure}\label{magnetosphere-structure}

\begin{figure}

\centering{

\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{chapters/../../media/images/fig-magnetosphere-structure.png}}

}

\caption{\label{fig-magnetosphere}The structure of Earth's
magnetosphere: solar wind compressed on the dayside creates the bow
shock and magnetopause, while the magnetotail extends away from the
Sun.}

\end{figure}%

\subsection{Key Regions}\label{key-regions}

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}ll@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
Region & Description \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
Bow shock & Where solar wind slows (supersonic \(\to\) subsonic) \\
Magnetopause & Boundary of magnetic dominance \\
Plasmasphere & Inner region of trapped plasma \\
Radiation belts & High-energy particle zones \\
Magnetotail & Extended tail (sunward side) \\
\end{longtable}

\section{The Solar Wind}\label{the-solar-wind}

Stream of charged particles from the Sun:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Speed}: 300-800 km/s
\item
  \textbf{Density}: \textasciitilde5 particles/cm³
\item
  \textbf{Composition}: Protons, electrons, some He
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Interaction with
Magnetosphere}\label{interaction-with-magnetosphere}

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  Solar wind approaches
\item
  Bow shock slows particles
\item
  Flow diverted around magnetopause
\item
  Some particles enter via cusps
\item
  Tail stretches in antisunward direction
\end{enumerate}

\section{Van Allen Belts}\label{van-allen-belts}

Trapped charged particle zones:

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Inner Belt (1-2
\(R_\oplus\))}{Inner Belt (1-2 R\_\textbackslash oplus)}}\label{inner-belt-1-2-r_oplus}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Primarily protons
\item
  Energies: 10-100 MeV
\item
  Source: Cosmic rays, solar protons
\end{itemize}

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Outer Belt (3-6
\(R_\oplus\))}{Outer Belt (3-6 R\_\textbackslash oplus)}}\label{outer-belt-3-6-r_oplus}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Primarily electrons
\item
  Energies: 0.1-10 MeV
\item
  Source: Solar wind injection
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Hazards}\label{hazards}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Damage to electronics
\item
  Radiation exposure for astronauts
\item
  Satellites must navigate carefully
\end{itemize}

\section{Aurora}\label{aurora}

When solar particles enter the atmosphere, they follow magnetic field
lines toward the poles. Upon colliding with atmospheric gases, they
excite atoms which then emit light, creating the aurora.

\subsection{Colors}\label{colors}

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}ll@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
Color & Cause \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
Green & Oxygen (557.7 nm) \\
Red & Oxygen (630.0 nm) \\
Blue/Purple & Nitrogen \\
\end{longtable}

\subsection{Locations}\label{locations}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Auroral ovals around magnetic poles
\item
  Expand during magnetic storms
\item
  Visible from 60-70° latitude typically
\end{itemize}

\section{Magnetic Reversals}\label{magnetic-reversals}

Earth's field flips polarity:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Average interval: \textasciitilde300,000 years
\item
  Last reversal: 780,000 years ago
\item
  Transition takes: \textasciitilde1,000-10,000 years
\item
  Field weakens during reversal
\end{itemize}

\section{Without a Magnetosphere}\label{without-a-magnetosphere}

\subsection{Mars}\label{mars-2}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Lost its magnetic field \textasciitilde4 billion years ago
\item
  Solar wind strips atmosphere
\item
  Surface now irradiated
\item
  May have prevented life
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Implications}\label{implications}

Magnetospheres are crucial for: - Atmospheric retention - Surface
habitability - Protection from cosmic rays

\section{In Simulation}\label{in-simulation-10}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\KeywordTok{class}\NormalTok{ Magnetosphere:}
    \KeywordTok{def} \FunctionTok{\_\_init\_\_}\NormalTok{(}\VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{, planet):}
        \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.planet }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ planet}
        \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.field\_strength }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ dynamo\_strength(planet.core)}

    \KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ shape(}\VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{, solar\_wind):}
        \CommentTok{\# Pressure balance determines magnetopause}
\NormalTok{        r\_magnetopause }\OperatorTok{=} \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.standoff\_distance(solar\_wind.pressure)}

        \CommentTok{\# Tail extends antisunward}
        \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.tail\_length }\OperatorTok{=} \DecValTok{100} \OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ r\_magnetopause}

    \KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ deflect(}\VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{, particle):}
        \ControlFlowTok{if}\NormalTok{ particle.charge }\OperatorTok{!=} \DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{:}
            \CommentTok{\# Lorentz force}
\NormalTok{            F }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ particle.charge }\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ cross(particle.velocity, }\VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.B\_field)}
\NormalTok{            particle.apply\_force(F)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\section{Concepts}\label{concepts-18}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{dynamo}: Mechanism generating magnetic fields
\item
  \textbf{magnetopause}: Boundary of magnetic dominance
\item
  \textbf{van-allen-belts}: Trapped radiation zones
\item
  \textbf{aurora}: Light from atmospheric particle collisions
\end{itemize}

\section{Transition}\label{transition-18}

We have explored planets in detail. Now we scale up to the engines that
power solar systems: stars themselves.

\part{The Stellar Realm}

\chapter{Stellar Formation}\label{sec-stellar-formation}

\emph{``From nebula to star''}

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, leftrule=.75mm, title=\textcolor{quarto-callout-note-color}{\faInfo}\hspace{0.5em}{Key Revelation}, bottomrule=.15mm, opacityback=0, coltitle=black, arc=.35mm, colframe=quarto-callout-note-color-frame, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-note-color!10!white, colback=white, titlerule=0mm, breakable, bottomtitle=1mm, toptitle=1mm, rightrule=.15mm, toprule=.15mm, left=2mm, opacitybacktitle=0.6]

Stars are born when gravity defeats pressure in molecular clouds. The
same process that forms planets (accretion) operates at vastly larger
scales to ignite fusion.

\end{tcolorbox}

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, leftrule=.75mm, title=\textcolor{quarto-callout-note-color}{\faInfo}\hspace{0.5em}{How FTD Produces Stellar Formation}, bottomrule=.15mm, opacityback=0, coltitle=black, arc=.35mm, colframe=quarto-callout-note-color-frame, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-note-color!10!white, colback=white, titlerule=0mm, breakable, bottomtitle=1mm, toptitle=1mm, rightrule=.15mm, toprule=.15mm, left=2mm, opacitybacktitle=0.6]

Stellar formation in FTD follows from flux dynamics at large scales:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Gravitational instability} = regions where flux density
  exceeds local pressure support
\item
  \textbf{Jeans mass} emerges from the balance of gravitational flux
  gradients vs kinetic flux
\item
  \textbf{Fragmentation} occurs when cooling (flux dissipation) reduces
  Jeans mass
\item
  \textbf{Fusion ignition} happens when core flux density reaches
  nuclear threshold
\end{itemize}

The hierarchical structure of star-forming regions reflects the
scale-free nature of gravitational flux collapse.

\end{tcolorbox}

\section{Molecular Cloud Collapse}\label{molecular-cloud-collapse}

\subsection{Initial Conditions}\label{initial-conditions}

Stars form in giant molecular clouds: - Size: 10-100 parsecs - Mass:
\(10^4\)-\(10^6\) \(M_\odot\) - Temperature: 10-20 K - Composition:
\(\text{H}_2\), He, dust

\subsection{Trigger Mechanisms}\label{trigger-mechanisms}

Something must start the collapse: - \textbf{Supernova shockwave}:
Compression - \textbf{Cloud collision}: Density increase -
\textbf{Spiral arm compression}: Density wave - \textbf{Turbulence
decay}: Support removal

\subsection{Jeans Criterion}\label{jeans-criterion}

Collapse occurs when gravity overcomes thermal pressure:

\[
M > M_J = \left(\frac{5k_BT}{G\mu m_H}\right)^{3/2} \left(\frac{3}{4\pi\rho}\right)^{1/2}
\]

For typical molecular cloud: \(M_J \approx 10-100 \, M_\odot\)

\section{Fragmentation}\label{fragmentation}

Large clouds break into multiple protostars through hierarchical
fragmentation: the initial cloud subdivides into clumps, each of which
continues collapsing independently.

\begin{figure}

\centering{

\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{chapters/../../media/images/fig-star-formation.png}}

}

\caption{\label{fig-star-formation}Star formation from molecular cloud
collapse through fragmentation, accretion, and protostellar evolution to
main sequence.}

\end{figure}%

\section{Protostellar Evolution}\label{protostellar-evolution}

\subsection{Class 0 (0-10,000 years)}\label{class-0-0-10000-years}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Deeply embedded in cloud
\item
  Invisible at optical wavelengths
\item
  Accretion onto central object
\item
  Bipolar outflows begin
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Class I (10,000-100,000
years)}\label{class-i-10000-100000-years}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Envelope still infalling
\item
  Infrared bright
\item
  Disk forming
\item
  Outflows strengthen
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Class II (100,000-1 million
years)}\label{class-ii-100000-1-million-years}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  T Tauri star
\item
  Visible optically
\item
  Disk prominent
\item
  Planet formation begins
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Class III (\textgreater1 million
years)}\label{class-iii-1-million-years}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Disk dispersing
\item
  Pre-main sequence
\item
  Approaching hydrogen fusion
\item
  ``Weak-line'' T Tauri
\end{itemize}

\section{The Birth Line}\label{the-birth-line}

Stars become visible when they emerge from their natal cloud. On the
Hertzsprung-Russell diagram, protostars first appear along the birth
line, then contract along pre-main sequence tracks until they reach the
main sequence where hydrogen fusion begins.

\section{Star Formation Efficiency}\label{star-formation-efficiency}

Only 1-10\% of cloud mass becomes stars: - Stellar winds disperse gas -
Radiation pressure pushes dust - Supernovae clear remaining material

\section{Initial Mass Function}\label{initial-mass-function}

The distribution of stellar masses at birth:

\[
\frac{dN}{dM} \propto M^{-2.35}
\]

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Many more low-mass stars than high-mass
\item
  Few stars \textgreater{} 50 \(M_\odot\)
\item
  Peak around 0.1-0.5 \(M_\odot\)
\end{itemize}

\section{In Simulation}\label{in-simulation-11}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ form\_star(molecular\_cloud):}
    \ControlFlowTok{while}\NormalTok{ cloud.mass }\OperatorTok{\textgreater{}}\NormalTok{ jeans\_mass(cloud):}
        \CommentTok{\# Gravitational collapse}
\NormalTok{        cloud.contract()}

        \CommentTok{\# Temperature rises from compression}
\NormalTok{        cloud.temperature }\OperatorTok{+=}\NormalTok{ compression\_heating()}

        \CommentTok{\# Check for ignition}
        \ControlFlowTok{if}\NormalTok{ cloud.core\_temp }\OperatorTok{\textgreater{}}\NormalTok{ FUSION\_THRESHOLD:}
            \ControlFlowTok{return}\NormalTok{ MainSequenceStar(cloud)}

        \CommentTok{\# Fragment if unstable}
        \ControlFlowTok{if}\NormalTok{ should\_fragment(cloud):}
            \ControlFlowTok{return}\NormalTok{ [form\_star(fragment) }\ControlFlowTok{for}\NormalTok{ fragment }\KeywordTok{in}\NormalTok{ split(cloud)]}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\section{Concepts}\label{concepts-19}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{jeans-mass}: Minimum mass for collapse
\item
  \textbf{fragmentation}: Cloud breaking into multiple stars
\item
  \textbf{protostar}: Star before hydrogen fusion begins
\item
  \textbf{t-tauri}: Young pre-main-sequence star
\end{itemize}

\section{Transition}\label{transition-19}

Protostars become real stars when fusion ignites. Next
(Chapter~\ref{sec-main-sequence}), we explore the main sequence: where
stars spend most of their lives, steadily fusing hydrogen into helium
for billions of years.

The journey from molecular cloud to main-sequence star takes millions of
years: a geological instant on cosmic timescales, yet long enough for
complex processes of fragmentation, accretion, and disk formation that
determine not just stellar properties but also the planets that may form
alongside.

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, leftrule=.75mm, title=\textcolor{quarto-callout-tip-color}{\faLightbulb}\hspace{0.5em}{Related Topics}, bottomrule=.15mm, opacityback=0, coltitle=black, arc=.35mm, colframe=quarto-callout-tip-color-frame, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-tip-color!10!white, colback=white, titlerule=0mm, breakable, bottomtitle=1mm, toptitle=1mm, rightrule=.15mm, toprule=.15mm, left=2mm, opacitybacktitle=0.6]

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Main sequence}: Chapter~\ref{sec-main-sequence} describes
  stellar hydrogen burning
\item
  \textbf{Stellar nucleosynthesis}:
  Chapter~\ref{sec-stellar-nucleosynthesis} covers element formation in
  stars
\item
  \textbf{Stellar death}: Chapter~\ref{sec-stellar-death} explores how
  stars end their lives
\item
  \textbf{Gravity wells}: Chapter~\ref{sec-gravity-wells} explains the
  gravitational dynamics underlying collapse
\end{itemize}

\end{tcolorbox}

\chapter{Main Sequence}\label{sec-main-sequence}

\emph{``Where stars spend their lives''}

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, leftrule=.75mm, title=\textcolor{quarto-callout-note-color}{\faInfo}\hspace{0.5em}{Key Revelation}, bottomrule=.15mm, opacityback=0, coltitle=black, arc=.35mm, colframe=quarto-callout-note-color-frame, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-note-color!10!white, colback=white, titlerule=0mm, breakable, bottomtitle=1mm, toptitle=1mm, rightrule=.15mm, toprule=.15mm, left=2mm, opacitybacktitle=0.6]

A main sequence star is a self-regulating fusion reactor: gravity
squeezes, fusion pushes back. This equilibrium can last billions of
years.

\end{tcolorbox}

\section{Hydrostatic Equilibrium}\label{hydrostatic-equilibrium}

The balance that defines a star:

\begin{figure}

\centering{

\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{chapters/../../media/images/fig-hydrostatic-equilibrium.png}}

}

\caption{\label{fig-hydrostatic}Hydrostatic equilibrium in a star:
gravity pulls inward while pressure from fusion pushes outward, creating
a stable balance.}

\end{figure}%

At every point in a stable star, the inward gravitational force is
exactly balanced by the outward pressure from fusion reactions in the
core.

\[
\frac{dP}{dr} = -\frac{Gm(r)\rho}{r^2}
\]

At every point, pressure exactly balances gravity.

\section{Energy Generation}\label{energy-generation}

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Proton-Proton Chain (M \textless{} 1.5
\(M_\odot\))}{Proton-Proton Chain (M \textless{} 1.5 M\_\textbackslash odot)}}\label{proton-proton-chain-m-1.5-m_odot}

Dominant in Sun-like stars:

\[
4p \rightarrow {^4\text{He}} + 2e^+ + 2\nu_e + 26.7 \text{ MeV}
\]

Step by step:

\begin{verbatim}
p + p → D + e⁺ + νₑ     (slow: rate limiting)
D + p → ³He + γ
³He + ³He → ⁴He + 2p
\end{verbatim}

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{CNO Cycle (M \textgreater{} 1.5
\(M_\odot\))}{CNO Cycle (M \textgreater{} 1.5 M\_\textbackslash odot)}}\label{cno-cycle-m-1.5-m_odot}

Carbon, nitrogen, oxygen catalyze fusion:

\begin{verbatim}
¹²C + p → ¹³N + γ
¹³N → ¹³C + e⁺ + νₑ
¹³C + p → ¹⁴N + γ
¹⁴N + p → ¹⁵O + γ     (slow: rate limiting)
¹⁵O → ¹⁵N + e⁺ + νₑ
¹⁵N + p → ¹²C + ⁴He
\end{verbatim}

Net: 4p → ⁴He (carbon recycled)

\section{The Mass-Luminosity
Relation}\label{the-mass-luminosity-relation}

\[
L \propto M^{3.5}
\]

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}llll@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
Mass & Luminosity & Temp & Lifetime \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
0.1 \(M_\odot\) & 0.001 \(L_\odot\) & 3000 K & 10 trillion yr \\
0.5 \(M_\odot\) & 0.03 \(L_\odot\) & 4000 K & 50 billion yr \\
1 \(M_\odot\) & 1 \(L_\odot\) & 5800 K & 10 billion yr \\
2 \(M_\odot\) & 16 \(L_\odot\) & 8000 K & 1 billion yr \\
10 \(M_\odot\) & 3000 \(L_\odot\) & 20000 K & 30 million yr \\
50 \(M_\odot\) & 500000 \(L_\odot\) & 40000 K & 4 million yr \\
\end{longtable}

More massive stars exhibit significantly shorter main-sequence lifetimes
due to their elevated core temperatures and correspondingly higher
fusion rates.

\section{Spectral Classification}\label{spectral-classification}

Stars are classified by temperature:

\begin{verbatim}
O B A F G K M (L T Y for brown dwarfs)

"(Traditional mnemonic: O, B, A, F, G, K, M, L, T, Y, ordered by decreasing surface temperature)"
\end{verbatim}

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}llll@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
Class & Temperature & Color & Example \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
O & \textgreater30,000 K & Blue & ζ Ophiuchi \\
B & 10-30,000 K & Blue-white & Rigel \\
A & 7,500-10,000 K & White & Sirius \\
F & 6,000-7,500 K & Yellow-white & Procyon \\
G & 5,200-6,000 K & Yellow & Sun \\
K & 3,700-5,200 K & Orange & Arcturus \\
M & 2,400-3,700 K & Red & Betelgeuse \\
\end{longtable}

\section{The HR Diagram}\label{the-hr-diagram}

The fundamental tool of stellar astronomy:

\begin{figure}

\centering{

\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{chapters/../../media/images/fig-hr-diagram.png}}

}

\caption{\label{fig-hr-diagram}The Hertzsprung-Russell diagram:
luminosity versus temperature for stars. The main sequence runs
diagonally from hot, luminous O stars to cool, dim M stars.}

\end{figure}%

The main sequence is a diagonal band where luminosity correlates with
temperature. Stars spend most of their lives on this band, burning
hydrogen in their cores.

\section{Stellar Structure}\label{stellar-structure}

\subsection{Core}\label{core}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Fusion zone
\item
  15 million K (Sun)
\item
  25\% of radius
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Radiative Zone}\label{radiative-zone}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Energy transport by photons
\item
  Photons scatter, random walk
\item
  25-70\% of radius (depends on mass)
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Convective Zone}\label{convective-zone}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Energy transport by bulk motion
\item
  Hot material rises, cool sinks
\item
  Outer 30\% (Sun)
\end{itemize}

\section{Self-Regulation}\label{self-regulation}

The stellar thermostat:

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  Fusion rate drops → core contracts
\item
  Contraction heats core
\item
  Higher temperature → faster fusion
\item
  More pressure → expansion
\item
  Expansion cools core
\item
  Back to equilibrium
\end{enumerate}

\section{In Simulation}\label{in-simulation-12}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\KeywordTok{class}\NormalTok{ MainSequenceStar:}
    \KeywordTok{def} \FunctionTok{\_\_init\_\_}\NormalTok{(}\VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{, mass):}
        \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.mass }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ mass}
        \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.luminosity }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ mass}\OperatorTok{**}\FloatTok{3.5}
        \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.temperature }\OperatorTok{=} \DecValTok{5800} \OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ mass}\OperatorTok{**}\FloatTok{0.5}
        \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.lifetime }\OperatorTok{=} \FloatTok{10e9} \OperatorTok{/}\NormalTok{ mass}\OperatorTok{**}\FloatTok{2.5}  \CommentTok{\# years}

    \KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ evolve(}\VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{, dt):}
        \CommentTok{\# Burn hydrogen}
\NormalTok{        H\_burned }\OperatorTok{=} \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.luminosity }\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ dt }\OperatorTok{/}\NormalTok{ FUSION\_EFFICIENCY}
        \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.core\_hydrogen }\OperatorTok{{-}=}\NormalTok{ H\_burned}

        \CommentTok{\# Adjust structure}
        \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.maintain\_equilibrium()}

        \CommentTok{\# Check for end of main sequence}
        \ControlFlowTok{if} \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.core\_hydrogen }\OperatorTok{\textless{}=} \DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{:}
            \ControlFlowTok{return} \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.evolve\_off\_main\_sequence()}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{itemize}
