\KeywordTok{class}\NormalTok{ BlackHole:}
    \KeywordTok{def} \FunctionTok{\_\_init\_\_}\NormalTok{(}\VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{, mass, spin}\OperatorTok{=}\DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{):}
        \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.mass }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ mass}
        \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.spin }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ spin  }\CommentTok{\# 0 to 1}
        \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.r\_s }\OperatorTok{=} \DecValTok{2} \OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ G }\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ mass }\OperatorTok{/}\NormalTok{ c}\OperatorTok{**}\DecValTok{2}
        \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.r\_event }\OperatorTok{=} \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.r\_s }\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ (}\DecValTok{1} \OperatorTok{+}\NormalTok{ sqrt(}\DecValTok{1} \OperatorTok{{-}}\NormalTok{ spin}\OperatorTok{**}\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{)) }\OperatorTok{/} \DecValTok{2}

    \KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ capture(}\VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{, particle):}
\NormalTok{        r }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ distance(particle.position, }\VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.position)}
        \ControlFlowTok{if}\NormalTok{ r }\OperatorTok{\textless{}} \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.r\_event:}
            \CommentTok{\# Point of no return}
            \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.mass }\OperatorTok{+=}\NormalTok{ particle.mass}
\NormalTok{            particle.state }\OperatorTok{=} \DecValTok{0}  \CommentTok{\# Returns to void}
            \ControlFlowTok{return} \VariableTok{True}
        \ControlFlowTok{return} \VariableTok{False}

    \KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ hawking\_temperature(}\VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{):}
        \ControlFlowTok{return}\NormalTok{ HBAR }\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ c}\OperatorTok{**}\DecValTok{3} \OperatorTok{/}\NormalTok{ (}\DecValTok{8} \OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ pi }\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ G }\OperatorTok{*} \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.mass }\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ k\_B)}

    \KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ evaporation\_time(}\VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{):}
        \ControlFlowTok{return} \DecValTok{5120} \OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ pi }\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ G}\OperatorTok{**}\DecValTok{2} \OperatorTok{*} \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.mass}\OperatorTok{**}\DecValTok{3} \OperatorTok{/}\NormalTok{ (HBAR }\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ c}\OperatorTok{**}\DecValTok{4}\NormalTok{)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\section{Transition}\label{transition-33}

Black holes warp spacetime. When they merge, they send ripples through
the fabric of space: gravitational waves
(Chapter~\ref{sec-gravitational-waves}).

The resolution of the information paradox in FTD illustrates a central
theme: apparent contradictions often arise from incomplete
understanding. What seemed like a fundamental conflict between gravity
and quantum mechanics turns out to be a feature of how information is
encoded and retrieved in a discrete lattice.

\chapter{Gravitational Waves}\label{sec-gravitational-waves}

\emph{``Ripples in spacetime''}

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, leftrule=.75mm, title=\textcolor{quarto-callout-note-color}{\faInfo}\hspace{0.5em}{Key Insight}, bottomrule=.15mm, opacityback=0, coltitle=black, arc=.35mm, colframe=quarto-callout-note-color-frame, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-note-color!10!white, colback=white, titlerule=0mm, breakable, bottomtitle=1mm, toptitle=1mm, rightrule=.15mm, toprule=.15mm, left=2mm, opacitybacktitle=0.6]

Gravitational waves, propagating disturbances in the spacetime metric,
were predicted by general relativity (Einstein 1916) and first directly
detected by LIGO in 2015 (B. P. Abbott et al. 2016).

\end{tcolorbox}

\section{What Are Gravitational
Waves?}\label{what-are-gravitational-waves}

Ripples in the fabric of spacetime, caused by accelerating masses.

\subsection{Production}\label{production}

When masses accelerate asymmetrically: - Binary stars orbiting -
Supernovae (if asymmetric) - Black hole mergers - Cosmic strings
(hypothetical)

\subsection{Propagation}\label{propagation}

Travel at speed c through spacetime itself.

\section{Properties}\label{properties-20}

\subsection{Strain}\label{strain}

\[
h = \frac{\Delta L}{L} \approx 10^{-21}
\]

For LIGO detections: 1/1000th the diameter of a proton over 4 km.

\subsection{Polarization}\label{polarization}

Two modes: - \textbf{Plus (+)}: Stretches along one axis, compresses
perpendicular - \textbf{Cross (×)}: Rotated 45° from plus

\begin{figure}

\centering{

\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{chapters/../../media/images/fig-gw-overview.png}}

}

\caption{\label{fig-gw-overview}Gravitational wave polarization modes,
detection methods, and characteristic waveforms from binary mergers.}

\end{figure}%

\subsection{Speed}\label{speed}

The propagation speed was confirmed to equal \(c\) to within
\(10^{-15}\) by the multi-messenger observation of GW170817 (B. P.
Abbott et al. 2017).

\section{Sources}\label{sources-1}

\subsection{Binary Black Holes}\label{binary-black-holes}

The primary source class for current detectors:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  GW150914: First detection, \(36 + 29 \to 62\,M_\odot\) (B. P. Abbott
  et al. 2016)
\item
  Characteristic ``chirp'' waveform from inspiral
\item
  \textasciitilde90 confirmed events through O3 observing run (R. Abbott
  et al. 2023)
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Binary Neutron Stars}\label{binary-neutron-stars}

GW170817: - First neutron star merger detected - Electromagnetic
counterpart - Confirmed speed = c - Heavy element production

\subsection{Supermassive Binaries}\label{supermassive-binaries}

LISA (future) targets: - Merging galaxy centers - \(10^6\)-\(10^7\)
\(M_\odot\) - Millihertz frequencies

\subsection{Primordial}\label{primordial}

From early universe: - Inflation - Phase transitions - Cosmic strings

\section{The Waveform}\label{the-waveform}

\subsection{Inspiral}\label{inspiral}

As two compact objects spiral together, the gravitational wave frequency
increases following the characteristic ``chirp'' waveform:

\[
f \propto t^{-3/8}
\]

The frequency rises slowly at first, then accelerates rapidly in the
final moments before merger.

\subsection{Merger}\label{merger}

Maximum amplitude, complex dynamics, peak frequency.

\subsection{Ringdown}\label{ringdown}

Damped oscillations as final black hole settles: \[
h(t) \propto e^{-t/\tau} \cos(\omega t)
\]

\section{Detection}\label{detection}

\subsection{LIGO/Virgo}\label{ligovirgo}

Laser interferometers with perpendicular arms 4 km long. A laser beam is
split and sent down both arms, reflected by mirrors, and recombined.
Gravitational waves cause differential length changes that shift the
interference pattern.

Sensitivity reaches \(10^{-21}\) strain: detecting length changes of
1/1000th the diameter of a proton over 4 km.

\subsection{How It Works}\label{how-it-works}

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  Laser split into two arms
\item
  GW stretches one arm, compresses other
\item
  Light returns out of phase
\item
  Interference pattern changes
\end{enumerate}

\subsection{Detections}\label{detections}

Since 2015: - \textasciitilde90 confirmed events - Mostly BBH mergers -
Some BNS, BHNS

\section{Science from GW}\label{science-from-gw}

Relativity}\label{tests-of-general-relativity}

\item

\begin{Highlighting}[]
\KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ gravitational\_wave(binary\_system, distance):}
\NormalTok{    m1, m2 }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ binary\_system.masses}
\NormalTok{    chirp\_mass }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ (m1 }\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ m2)}\OperatorTok{**}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{3}\OperatorTok{/}\DecValTok{5}\NormalTok{) }\OperatorTok{/}\NormalTok{ (m1 }\OperatorTok{+}\NormalTok{ m2)}\OperatorTok{**}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{1}\OperatorTok{/}\DecValTok{5}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{    f }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ binary\_system.orbital\_frequency}

    \CommentTok{\# Strain amplitude}
\NormalTok{    h }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ (}\DecValTok{4} \OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ G }\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ chirp\_mass }\OperatorTok{/}\NormalTok{ c}\OperatorTok{**}\DecValTok{2} \OperatorTok{/}\NormalTok{ distance) }\OperatorTok{*} \OperatorTok{\textbackslash{}}
\NormalTok{        (pi }\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ G }\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ chirp\_mass }\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ f }\OperatorTok{/}\NormalTok{ c}\OperatorTok{**}\DecValTok{3}\NormalTok{)}\OperatorTok{**}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{2}\OperatorTok{/}\DecValTok{3}\NormalTok{)}

    \ControlFlowTok{return}\NormalTok{ h}

\KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ inspiral\_waveform(t, chirp\_mass):}
    \CommentTok{\# Frequency evolution (chirp formula)}
\NormalTok{    M }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ G }\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ chirp\_mass }\OperatorTok{/}\NormalTok{ c}\OperatorTok{**}\DecValTok{3}
\NormalTok{    f }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ (}\DecValTok{256}\OperatorTok{/}\DecValTok{5} \OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ M}\OperatorTok{**}\NormalTok{(}\OperatorTok{{-}}\DecValTok{5}\OperatorTok{/}\DecValTok{3}\NormalTok{) }\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ t)}\OperatorTok{**}\NormalTok{(}\OperatorTok{{-}}\DecValTok{3}\OperatorTok{/}\DecValTok{8}\NormalTok{)}

    \CommentTok{\# Phase}
\NormalTok{    phi }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ integrate(}\DecValTok{2} \OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ pi }\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ f, t)}

    \ControlFlowTok{return}\NormalTok{ h\_0 }\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ cos(phi)}
\end{Highlighting}

Gravitational waves come from the most violent events. But high-energy

\chapter{Cosmic Rays}\label{sec-cosmic-rays}

\emph{``High-energy messengers''}

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, leftrule=.75mm, title=\textcolor{quarto-callout-note-color}{\faInfo}\hspace{0.5em}{Key Revelation}, bottomrule=.15mm, opacityback=0, coltitle=black, arc=.35mm, colframe=quarto-callout-note-color-frame, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-note-color!10!white, colback=white, titlerule=0mm, breakable, bottomtitle=1mm, toptitle=1mm, rightrule=.15mm, toprule=.15mm, left=2mm, opacitybacktitle=0.6]

The universe is filled with speeding particles: some with energies far
beyond what our accelerators achieve. Cosmic rays are messengers from
the most violent events in the cosmos.

\end{tcolorbox}

\section{What Are Cosmic Rays?}\label{what-are-cosmic-rays}

High-energy particles from space, mostly protons and nuclei.

\subsection{Composition}\label{composition-1}

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}ll@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
Particle & Fraction \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
Protons & 87\% \\
Helium nuclei & 12\% \\
Heavier nuclei & 1\% \\
Electrons & 0.1\% \\
\end{longtable}

\subsection{Discovery}\label{discovery-1}

Victor Hess (1912): - Balloon experiments - Ionization increased with
altitude - Must come from above - Nobel Prize 1936

\section{The Energy Spectrum}\label{the-energy-spectrum}

\[
\frac{dN}{dE} \propto E^{-\gamma}
\]

\subsection{Features}\label{features}

\begin{figure}

\centering{

\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{chapters/../../media/images/fig-cosmic-ray-spectrum.png}}

}

\caption{\label{fig-cosmic-ray-spectrum}The cosmic ray energy spectrum
showing three distinctive features: the ``knee'' at 3×10¹⁵ eV where the
spectrum steepens, the ``ankle'' at 3×10¹⁸ eV where it flattens, and the
GZK cutoff at 5×10¹⁹ eV.}

\end{figure}%

The spectrum follows a power law with spectral index γ that changes at
characteristic energies: γ ≈ 2.7 below the knee, steepening to γ ≈ 3.0
between knee and ankle, then returning to γ ≈ 2.7 above the ankle before
the GZK cutoff.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{The Knee (\(3 \times 10^{15}\)
eV)}{The Knee (3 \textbackslash times 10\^{}\{15\} eV)}}\label{the-knee-3-times-1015-ev}

Spectrum steepens: - Galactic accelerators reach limit? - Transition
region?

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{The Ankle (\(3 \times 10^{18}\)
eV)}{The Ankle (3 \textbackslash times 10\^{}\{18\} eV)}}\label{the-ankle-3-times-1018-ev}

Spectrum flattens: - Extragalactic sources dominate - Transition from
galactic to extragalactic

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{GZK Cutoff (\(5 \times 10^{19}\)
eV)}{GZK Cutoff (5 \textbackslash times 10\^{}\{19\} eV)}}\label{gzk-cutoff-5-times-1019-ev}

Interaction with CMB: \[
p + \gamma_{\text{CMB}} \rightarrow \Delta^+ \rightarrow p + \pi^0 \text{ or } n + \pi^+
\]

Limits how far UHECRs can travel (\textasciitilde100 Mpc).

\section{Acceleration}\label{acceleration}

\subsection{Fermi Acceleration}\label{fermi-acceleration}

\subsubsection{First Order (Shock
Acceleration)}\label{first-order-shock-acceleration}

At supernova shocks: 1. Particle crosses shock 2. Scattered by magnetic
fields 3. Returns across shock 4. Gains energy each crossing

\[
\frac{\Delta E}{E} \approx \frac{v_{\text{shock}}}{c}
\]

Maximum energy: \[
E_{\text{max}} \propto Z B R v_{\text{shock}}
\]

\subsection{Hillas Criterion}\label{hillas-criterion}

Sources must satisfy: \[
E_{\text{max}} = Z e B R \beta c
\]

Larger B or R → higher energy possible.

\subsection{Candidate Sources}\label{candidate-sources}

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}lll@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
Source & Max Energy & Notes \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
SNR & \(10^{15}\) eV & Galactic knee \\
Pulsars & \(10^{16}\) eV & Strong B field \\
AGN jets & \(10^{20}\) eV & UHECR sources? \\
GRBs & \(10^{20}\) eV & Transient \\
\end{longtable}

\section{Detection}\label{detection-1}

\subsection{Direct (Low Energy)}\label{direct-low-energy}

Satellites and balloons: - Measure particle directly - Limited
collecting area - Works up to \textasciitilde{}\(10^{15}\) eV

\subsection{Indirect (High Energy)}\label{indirect-high-energy}

Ground arrays: - Particle showers in atmosphere - Spread over km² -
Thousands of detectors

\subsection{Air Showers}\label{air-showers}

When a primary cosmic ray strikes the atmosphere, it triggers a cascade:
the primary collides with an atmospheric nucleus, producing pions and
other hadrons. These decay into muons, electrons, and photons, which
multiply through further interactions. The shower spreads laterally as
it descends, forming a cone of billions of secondary particles that
reach ground level spread over kilometers.

\section{Ultra-High Energy Cosmic
Rays}\label{ultra-high-energy-cosmic-rays}

The highest energy particles known: - \(10^{20}\) eV (= 50 J in one
particle!) - Tennis ball at 100 km/h: in one proton - Origin: Still
debated

\subsection{The Oh-My-God Particle
(1991)}\label{the-oh-my-god-particle-1991}

\[
E = 3.2 \times 10^{20} \text{ eV}
\]

Single proton with macroscopic energy.

\section{In Simulation}\label{in-simulation-24}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\KeywordTok{class}\NormalTok{ CosmicRay:}
    \KeywordTok{def} \FunctionTok{\_\_init\_\_}\NormalTok{(}\VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{, energy, particle\_type}\OperatorTok{=}\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}proton\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{):}
        \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.energy }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ energy}
        \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.}\BuiltInTok{type} \OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ particle\_type}

    \KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ propagate(}\VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{):}
        \ControlFlowTok{while} \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.energy }\OperatorTok{\textgreater{}}\NormalTok{ threshold:}
            \CommentTok{\# GZK losses for UHECR}
            \ControlFlowTok{if} \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.energy }\OperatorTok{\textgreater{}}\NormalTok{ GZK\_ENERGY:}
                \ControlFlowTok{if}\NormalTok{ interacts\_with\_cmb():}
                    \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.energy }\OperatorTok{{-}=}\NormalTok{ pion\_production\_loss()}

            \CommentTok{\# Magnetic deflection}
\NormalTok{            deflection }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ z }\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ B\_field }\OperatorTok{/} \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.energy}
            \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.direction }\OperatorTok{+=}\NormalTok{ random\_deflection(deflection)}

    \KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ air\_shower(}\VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{, atmosphere):}
\NormalTok{        cascade }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ []}
\NormalTok{        cascade.append(}\VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{)}

        \ControlFlowTok{while}\NormalTok{ cascade:}
\NormalTok{            particle }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ cascade.pop()}
\NormalTok{            secondaries }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ particle.interact(atmosphere)}
\NormalTok{            cascade.extend(secondaries)}

        \ControlFlowTok{return}\NormalTok{ count\_at\_ground(cascade)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\section{Transition}\label{transition-35}

Even ``empty'' space is not really empty. Quantum fluctuations create

\chapter{Vacuum Fluctuations}\label{sec-vacuum-fluctuations}

\emph{``The quantum foam''}

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, leftrule=.75mm, title=\textcolor{quarto-callout-note-color}{\faInfo}\hspace{0.5em}{Key Revelation}, bottomrule=.15mm, opacityback=0, coltitle=black, arc=.35mm, colframe=quarto-callout-note-color-frame, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-note-color!10!white, colback=white, titlerule=0mm, breakable, bottomtitle=1mm, toptitle=1mm, rightrule=.15mm, toprule=.15mm, left=2mm, opacitybacktitle=0.6]

Empty space is not empty. Quantum mechanics guarantees that even the
vacuum churns with virtual particles, popping in and out of existence.
The Void is never truly still.

\end{tcolorbox}

\section{The Quantum Vacuum}\label{the-quantum-vacuum}

\subsection{Zero-Point Energy}\label{zero-point-energy}

Every quantum field has minimum energy, even in its ground state: \[
E = \frac{1}{2}\hbar\omega \text{ per mode}
\]

Summing over all modes → infinite energy (requires regularization).

\subsection{Virtual Particles}\label{virtual-particles}

\begin{figure}

\centering{

\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{chapters/../../media/images/fig-vacuum-fluctuations.png}}

}

\caption{\label{fig-vacuum-fluctuations}Vacuum fluctuations: virtual
particle-antiparticle pairs constantly appear and disappear, borrowing
energy briefly from the vacuum.}

\end{figure}%

Pairs constantly appear and disappear, existing for a time Δt ≈ ℏ/2ΔE
before annihilating. Heisenberg uncertainty allows: \[
\Delta E \cdot \Delta t \geq \frac{\hbar}{2}
\]

Borrow energy briefly, pay it back.

\section{Observable Effects}\label{observable-effects}

\subsection{Casimir Effect}\label{casimir-effect}

Two parallel plates attract:

\[
\frac{F}{A} = -\frac{\pi^2 \hbar c}{240 d^4}
\]

\subsection{Why?}\label{why}

Between the plates, only wavelengths that fit an integer number of
half-wavelengths can exist. Outside, all wavelengths are permitted. This
means more vacuum modes exist outside than inside, creating a pressure
difference that pushes the plates together.

Measured experimentally to \textasciitilde1\% precision.

\subsection{Lamb Shift}\label{lamb-shift}

Hydrogen energy levels shifted from naive prediction: - 2S and 2P levels
not degenerate - Caused by electron interacting with virtual photons -
Confirmed quantum electrodynamics

Shift: \textasciitilde1000 MHz

\subsection{Anomalous Magnetic Moment}\label{anomalous-magnetic-moment}

Electron g-factor: \[
g = 2.002319...
\]

The 0.002\ldots{} comes from virtual particle interactions.

Most precisely verified prediction in physics.

\section{The Cosmological Constant
Problem}\label{the-cosmological-constant-problem-1}

Naïve vacuum energy calculation: \[
\rho_{\text{QFT}} \approx M_{\text{Planck}}^4 \approx 10^{76} \text{ GeV}^4
\]

Observed dark energy: \[
\rho_{\Lambda} \approx 10^{-47} \text{ GeV}^4
\]

Discrepancy: \textasciitilde{}\(10^{120}\)

The worst prediction in physics.

\section{Planck Scale Foam}\label{planck-scale-foam}

At the Planck scale: \[
\ell_P = \sqrt{\frac{\hbar G}{c^3}} \approx 10^{-35} \text{ m}
\]

Spacetime itself may fluctuate: - Geometry uncertain - ``Foam''-like
structure - Virtual black holes?

\subsection{Implications}\label{implications-1}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Smooth spacetime is approximation
\item
  May affect ultra-high energy physics
\item
  Quantum gravity territory
\end{itemize}

\section{Dynamical Casimir Effect}\label{dynamical-casimir-effect}

Moving mirrors can create real particles: - Accelerate mirror fast
enough - Virtual photon has nowhere to go - Becomes real photon

Experimentally observed (2011).

\section{Hawking Radiation Redux}\label{hawking-radiation-redux}

When a virtual particle-antiparticle pair forms near the event horizon,
tidal forces can separate them before they annihilate. One particle
falls into the black hole while the other escapes to infinity, becoming
a real particle. The escaping particle carries energy away, causing the
black hole to slowly evaporate.

\section{Transition}\label{transition-36}

We have explored extreme phenomena. Now we step back to see how
complexity emerges from simple rules: self-organization, entropy, and
the nature of emergence.

\part{Emergent Phenomena}

\chapter{The Definition of Life}\label{sec-definition-life}

\emph{``What is it to be alive, precisely?''}

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, leftrule=.75mm, title=\textcolor{quarto-callout-note-color}{\faInfo}\hspace{0.5em}{Purpose of This Chapter}, bottomrule=.15mm, opacityback=0, coltitle=black, arc=.35mm, colframe=quarto-callout-note-color-frame, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-note-color!10!white, colback=white, titlerule=0mm, breakable, bottomtitle=1mm, toptitle=1mm, rightrule=.15mm, toprule=.15mm, left=2mm, opacitybacktitle=0.6]

This chapter provides an \textbf{operational definition of life} in FTD
terms. We distinguish life from non-life using the framework's
primitives: states, flux, entropy, and feedback. This grounds the
emergence discussion in the chapters that follow.

\end{tcolorbox}

\section{The Gap We Must Bridge}\label{the-gap-we-must-bridge}

FTD derives physics from the lattice up: - Particles from manifestation
- Atoms from binding - Molecules from chemistry - Materials from
aggregation

But somewhere in this hierarchy, \textbf{life} appears. What
distinguishes: - A rock (not alive) from a bacterium (alive)? - A dead
cell from a living cell? - A complex machine from an organism?

We need a definition that is: 1. \textbf{Precise}: Stated in FTD terms
2. \textbf{Operational}: Decidable in principle 3.
\textbf{Non-arbitrary}: Captures what we mean by ``life''

\section{What Life Is Not}\label{what-life-is-not}

\subsection{Not Complexity Alone}\label{not-complexity-alone}

A hurricane is complex: it has structure, dynamics, and information
flow. But a hurricane is not alive. Complexity is necessary but not
sufficient.

\subsection{Not Self-Replication
Alone}\label{not-self-replication-alone}

Fire spreads and ``reproduces.'' Crystals grow. Prions copy themselves.
But none of these are alive in the full sense. Replication is necessary
but not sufficient.

\subsection{Not Carbon-Based}\label{not-carbon-based}

This is chemistry-dependent, not fundamental. Silicon life, if it
exists, would still be life.

\subsection{Not ``Has DNA''}\label{not-has-dna}

DNA is one implementation of genetic memory. It is not the definition.

\section{The FTD Definition of Life}\label{the-ftd-definition-of-life}

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, leftrule=.75mm, title=\textcolor{quarto-callout-important-color}{\faExclamation}\hspace{0.5em}{Definition: Life}, bottomrule=.15mm, opacityback=0, coltitle=black, arc=.35mm, colframe=quarto-callout-important-color-frame, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-important-color!10!white, colback=white, titlerule=0mm, breakable, bottomtitle=1mm, toptitle=1mm, rightrule=.15mm, toprule=.15mm, left=2mm, opacitybacktitle=0.6]

A \textbf{living system} is a spatially localized pattern of manifested
states \(\{s(v)\}\) that:

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Maintains organization against entropy} by continuous flux
  exchange with environment
\item
  \textbf{Contains a self-model} (representation of its own structure
  encoded in its configuration)
\item
  \textbf{Uses the self-model to guide behavior} (feedback loop that
  references the model)
\item
  \textbf{Propagates the pattern} (creates copies or successors that
  inherit the self-model)
\end{enumerate}

\end{tcolorbox}

Let us examine each criterion.

\section{Criterion 1: Maintenance Against
Entropy}\label{criterion-1-maintenance-against-entropy}

\subsection{The Second Law}\label{the-second-law}

Isolated systems evolve toward maximum entropy. Structure dissipates.
Order degrades.

\subsection{The Living Exception}\label{the-living-exception}

Living systems \textbf{locally decrease entropy} by: - Importing
low-entropy energy (food, sunlight) - Exporting high-entropy waste
(heat, CO2) - Using the difference to maintain structure

In FTD terms:
\[\frac{d S_{\text{local}}}{dt} < 0 \quad \text{while} \quad \frac{d S_{\text{total}}}{dt} > 0\]

The organism stays ordered; the universe becomes more disordered
overall.

\subsection{Operational Test}\label{operational-test}

A system satisfies Criterion 1 if: - Cut off from flux exchange, it
degrades (death) - With flux exchange, it maintains structure
indefinitely

A rock fails: it does not need flux exchange to persist. A fire fails:
it does not maintain a specific structure. A cell passes: cut off from
nutrients, it dies; with nutrients, it maintains.

\section{Criterion 2: Self-Model}\label{criterion-2-self-model}

\subsection{What Is a Self-Model?}\label{what-is-a-self-model}

A \textbf{self-model} is a configuration within the system that
represents the system's structure:

\[\phi: \Omega \to \mathcal{M}\]

where: - \(\Omega\) is the system's physical substrate - \(\mathcal{M}\)
is the representation space - \(\phi\) encodes information about
\(\Omega\)'s structure

\subsection{Examples}\label{examples-3}

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}ll@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
System & Self-Model \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
Bacterium & DNA (encodes proteins that build the cell) \\
Animal & Genome + nervous system (body schema) \\
Ecosystem & Species distributions + nutrient cycles \\
\end{longtable}

\subsection{The Minimum}\label{the-minimum}

The minimal self-model is \textbf{genetic information}: instructions for
building a copy of the structure.

In FTD terms: a pattern of states that, when ``read'' by the system's
dynamics, produces a copy of that pattern along with the rest of the
organism.

\section{Criterion 3: Feedback from
Self-Model}\label{criterion-3-feedback-from-self-model}

\subsection{Model-Guided Behavior}\label{model-guided-behavior}

Having a self-model is not enough. The system must \textbf{use} the
model:

\[\text{Action}(t+1) = f(\text{State}(t), \phi(\Omega))\]

Behavior depends on both current state AND the self-model.

\subsection{Examples}\label{examples-4}

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 2\tabcolsep) * \real{0.2963}}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 2\tabcolsep) * \real{0.7037}}@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
\begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
System
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
Feedback Mechanism
\end{minipage} \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
Cell & Gene expression responds to cellular state \\
Immune system & Antibodies target non-self (requires self/non-self
model) \\
Brain & Motor commands reference body schema \\
\end{longtable}

\subsection{Why This Matters}\label{why-this-matters}

A crystal has structure but no self-model. A computer has data about
itself but does not use it to maintain itself (unless programmed to).
Life uses its self-model for self-maintenance.

\section{Criterion 4: Pattern
Propagation}\label{criterion-4-pattern-propagation}

\subsection{Inheritance}\label{inheritance}

Living systems produce successors that: - Have similar structure
(inheritance) - Contain the self-model (or a copy) - Can themselves
satisfy Criteria 1-3

\subsection{Variation}\label{variation}

Copies need not be exact. Mutation, variation, and evolution are
permitted. What matters is: - The self-model is transmitted - Successors
can be living systems

\subsection{Edge Cases}\label{edge-cases}

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 4\tabcolsep) * \real{0.2727}}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 4\tabcolsep) * \real{0.3636}}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 4\tabcolsep) * \real{0.3636}}@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
\begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
Case
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
Alive?
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
Reason
\end{minipage} \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
Sterile organism & Yes & Satisfies 1-3; propagation is capacity, not
achievement \\
Virus & Borderline & Has self-model but requires host for 1 and 3 \\
Mule & Yes & Satisfies 1-3; hybrid origin does not negate life \\
\end{longtable}

\section{The Formal Definition}\label{the-formal-definition}

Putting it together:

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, leftrule=.75mm, title=\textcolor{quarto-callout-important-color}{\faExclamation}\hspace{0.5em}{Definition (Formal)}, bottomrule=.15mm, opacityback=0, coltitle=black, arc=.35mm, colframe=quarto-callout-important-color-frame, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-important-color!10!white, colback=white, titlerule=0mm, breakable, bottomtitle=1mm, toptitle=1mm, rightrule=.15mm, toprule=.15mm, left=2mm, opacitybacktitle=0.6]

A configuration \(\Omega \subset \mathcal{L}\) is \textbf{living} at
time \(t\) if:

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\item
  \(\exists\) continuous flux exchange
  \(\mathbf{J}_{\text{in}}, \mathbf{J}_{\text{out}}\) maintaining
  \(S(\Omega) < S_{\text{max}}\)
\item
  \(\exists\) subconfiguration \(\Omega_\phi \subset \Omega\) encoding
  \(\phi: \Omega \to \mathcal{M}\)
\item
  The dynamics satisfy:
  \(s(\Omega, t+1) = F(s(\Omega, t), \phi(\Omega))\) (feedback)
\item
  Under favorable conditions, \(\Omega\) produces \(\Omega'\) with
  \(\phi' \approx \phi\) (propagation)
\end{enumerate}

\end{tcolorbox}

\section{The Void Cycle of Life}\label{the-void-cycle-of-life}

Life follows the pattern:

\[0 \xrightarrow{\text{genesis}} \pm 1 \xrightarrow{\text{maintenance}} \pm 1 \xrightarrow{\text{death}} 0\]

More specifically:

\begin{verbatim}
BIRTH:    Flux concentrates → Manifestation → Organization
LIFE:     Flux exchange → Self-maintenance → Self-replication
DEATH:    Flux disperses → Structure degrades → Return to void
\end{verbatim}

Every living thing is a temporary excursion from void, sustained by
flux, and destined to return.

\section{Life in the Computational
Hierarchy}\label{life-in-the-computational-hierarchy}

Connecting to the computational ontology (Ch. 14.7):

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}lll@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
Level & Gates & Life Status \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
Dead & 0 & No flux exchange, no maintenance \\
Brain Dead & 1 & Detection only, no self-model \\
Living (minimal) & \(\geq 2\) & Self-model + feedback + maintenance \\
Conscious & \(\geq 2\) + sLoop & Self-model of self-model \\
\end{longtable}

Life requires at least the capacity for feedback (2+ gates), but life
itself is defined by the maintenance + self-model + propagation
structure, not by computational capacity alone.

\section{Abiogenesis: The Origin of
Life}\label{abiogenesis-the-origin-of-life}

How does life arise from non-life?

In FTD terms: 1. Complex chemistry creates autocatalytic cycles
(Criterion 1 precursor) 2. Information-bearing polymers emerge
(Criterion 2 precursor) 3. Coupling between catalysis and information
creates feedback (Criterion 3) 4. Template replication enables
propagation (Criterion 4)

The transition is \textbf{gradual}: protocells satisfy some criteria
partially before full life emerges.

\section{What This Definition
Excludes}\label{what-this-definition-excludes}

\subsection{Excluded: Corporations, Nations,
Ideas}\label{excluded-corporations-nations-ideas}

These are patterns of behavior among living things, not living things
themselves. They lack: - Physical substrate undergoing flux exchange -
Continuous maintenance against entropy at the pattern level

\subsection{Excluded: AI (Currently)}\label{excluded-ai-currently}

Current AI systems: - Have no physical self-model (their code is not
``about'' their hardware) - Do not maintain themselves against entropy -
Do not propagate copies that inherit the self-model

This could change with different architectures.

\subsection{Included: Hypothetical Silicon
Life}\label{included-hypothetical-silicon-life}

If a silicon-based system satisfied all four criteria, it would be alive
by this definition.

\section{Concepts}\label{concepts-36}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{life-definition}: Maintenance + self-model + feedback +
  propagation
\item
  \textbf{entropy-maintenance}: Local entropy decrease through flux
  exchange
\item
  \textbf{self-model}: Internal representation of system structure
\item
  \textbf{feedback-loop}: Behavior depends on self-model
\item
  \textbf{void-cycle}: Birth → life → death as flux
  concentration/dispersal
\item
  \textbf{abiogenesis}: Gradual transition from non-life to life
\end{itemize}

\section{Transition}\label{transition-37}

With life defined in FTD terms, we can now examine how living systems
self-organize, how they encode and process information, and how
consciousness emerges from sufficiently complex life (see Ch. 21 for the
formal treatment). The following chapters trace this arc from simple
self-organization to the mystery of awareness.

\chapter{Self-Organization}\label{sec-self-organization}

\emph{``Order from chaos''}

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, leftrule=.75mm, title=\textcolor{quarto-callout-note-color}{\faInfo}\hspace{0.5em}{Key Revelation}, bottomrule=.15mm, opacityback=0, coltitle=black, arc=.35mm, colframe=quarto-callout-note-color-frame, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-note-color!10!white, colback=white, titlerule=0mm, breakable, bottomtitle=1mm, toptitle=1mm, rightrule=.15mm, toprule=.15mm, left=2mm, opacitybacktitle=0.6]

Complexity does not require a designer. Simple local rules, repeated
endlessly, can create structures of breathtaking intricacy. The universe
organizes itself.

\end{tcolorbox}

\section{Patterns from Simple Rules}\label{patterns-from-simple-rules}

\subsection{The Surprise of Emergence}\label{the-surprise-of-emergence}

When you follow simple rules: - No individual knows the pattern - No
central control exists - Yet structure appears

This is self-organization.

\subsection{Cellular Automata}\label{cellular-automata}

Local rules → global patterns.

\subsubsection{Conway's Game of Life}\label{conways-game-of-life}

Four simple rules govern each cell's fate:

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Underpopulation}: fewer than 2 neighbors causes death
\item
  \textbf{Survival}: 2-3 neighbors allows survival
\item
  \textbf{Overpopulation}: more than 3 neighbors causes death
\item
  \textbf{Reproduction}: exactly 3 neighbors triggers birth
\end{enumerate}

From four rules: gliders, oscillators, and Turing-complete computation.

\begin{figure}

\centering{

\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{chapters/../../media/images/fig-self-organization-examples.png}}

}

\caption{\label{fig-self-org-examples}Examples of self-organization from
simple rules: cellular automata patterns, convection cells, and other
emergent structures.}

\end{figure}%

\subsection{Reaction-Diffusion}\label{reaction-diffusion}

Turing patterns from activator-inhibitor dynamics: \[
\frac{\partial u}{\partial t} = D_u \nabla^2 u + f(u,v)
\] \[
\frac{\partial v}{\partial t} = D_v \nabla^2 v + g(u,v)
\]

Creates spots, stripes, spirals: the patterns of life.

\subsection{Flocking (Boids)}\label{flocking-boids}

Reynolds' three rules: 1. \textbf{Separation}: Avoid crowding neighbors
2. \textbf{Alignment}: Match heading with neighbors 3.
\textbf{Cohesion}: Move toward average position

Result: Emergent flocking behavior indistinguishable from birds.

\section{Dissipative Structures}\label{dissipative-structures}

\subsection{Far from Equilibrium}\label{far-from-equilibrium}

Prigogine's insight: Order can emerge when: - Open system (energy flows
through) - Far from equilibrium - Nonlinear dynamics - Fluctuations
present

\subsection{Bénard Cells}\label{buxe9nard-cells}

Heat a fluid from below. When the temperature gradient exceeds a
critical threshold, hexagonal convection cells appear spontaneously:
warm fluid rises in the center, cools at the top, and descends at the
edges. No blueprint, no designer, just physics organizing itself.

\subsection{Chemical Oscillators}\label{chemical-oscillators}

BZ reaction creates traveling waves: - No stirring - No external forcing
- Just chemistry organizing itself

\subsection{Hurricanes}\label{hurricanes}

Self-organized heat engine: - Warm ocean provides energy - Coriolis
force creates spin - Spiral structure emerges - Maintains itself against
entropy

\section{Self-Organized Criticality}\label{self-organized-criticality}

\subsection{The Sandpile Model}\label{the-sandpile-model}

Add grains one at a time. The pile grows until it reaches a critical
slope, after which avalanches of all sizes occur with a power-law
distribution. The system finds its own critical point without external
tuning.

\subsection{Properties}\label{properties-21}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{No tuning needed}: System finds critical point
\item
  \textbf{Power laws}: \(P(\text{size}) \propto \text{size}^{-\tau}\)
\item
  \textbf{Scale-free}: No characteristic size
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Examples in Nature}\label{examples-in-nature}

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}ll@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
System & Avalanche \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
Earthquakes & Magnitude distribution \\
Forest fires & Burn area \\
Extinctions & Species lost \\
Neural activity & Cascade size \\
Solar flares & Energy release \\
\end{longtable}

\section{In the FTD Framework}\label{in-the-ftd-framework-1}

Self-organization emerges naturally:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ self\_organization\_demo(grid):}
    \CommentTok{\# No special rules needed}
    \CommentTok{\# Just run the causal loop}

    \ControlFlowTok{for}\NormalTok{ tick }\KeywordTok{in} \BuiltInTok{range}\NormalTok{(many\_ticks):}
        \CommentTok{\# Local interactions only}
        \ControlFlowTok{for}\NormalTok{ voxel }\KeywordTok{in}\NormalTok{ grid:}
\NormalTok{            compute\_forces(voxel, neighbors)}
\NormalTok{            apply\_forces(voxel)}
\NormalTok{            move(voxel)}

        \CommentTok{\# Detect emergent structures}
\NormalTok{        triads }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ find\_triads(grid)}
\NormalTok{        clusters }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ find\_clusters(grid)}
\NormalTok{        patterns }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ analyze\_patterns(grid)}

\end{Highlighting}

\subsection{Why It Works}\label{why-it-works}

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Local rules}: Each voxel only sees neighbors
\item
  \textbf{Nonlinearity}: Threshold for manifestation (KB)
\item
  \textbf{Feedback}: Forces create correlations
\item
  \textbf{Energy flow}: Flux propagation
\end{enumerate}

\subsection{Emergent Scales}\label{emergent-scales}

From local rules: - Quarks → Hadrons - Hadrons → Nuclei - Nuclei +
Electrons → Atoms - Atoms → Molecules - Molecules → Crystals, Cells -
Cells → Organisms - Stars → Galaxies

No level ``knows'' about levels above.

\section{The Edge of Chaos}\label{the-edge-of-chaos}

\subsection{Phase Diagram of
Complexity}\label{phase-diagram-of-complexity}

Between frozen order and random chaos lies a critical point where
complexity is maximized. Too much order produces static patterns. Too
much chaos produces noise. At the edge, computation becomes possible.

\subsection{Computation at the Edge}\label{computation-at-the-edge}

Complex computation requires: - Enough structure to store information -
Enough dynamics to process it - Balance: the edge of chaos

\section{Experiments}\label{experiments-37}

\subsection{Flocking Simulation}\label{flocking-simulation}

Watch simple boid rules create emergent flocking behavior.

\subsection{Sandpile Dynamics}\label{sandpile-dynamics}

Add grains, observe power-law avalanche distribution.

\subsection{Pattern Formation}\label{pattern-formation}

See Turing patterns emerge from reaction-diffusion.

\section{Concepts}\label{concepts-37}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{emergence}: Wholes with properties absent in parts
\item
  \textbf{self-organized-criticality}: Spontaneous power laws
\item
  \textbf{dissipative-structures}: Order from energy flow
\item
  \textbf{edge-of-chaos}: Complexity sweet spot
\end{itemize}

\section{Transition}\label{transition-38}

Order emerges from chaos. But what is order, really? What distinguishes
organized from disorganized? Next
(Chapter~\ref{sec-information-entropy}), we explore information and
entropy: the deep mathematical structures that quantify organization.

FTD itself exemplifies self-organization: from the simplest possible
rules (local updates on a ternary lattice), the entire complexity of the
universe emerges without top-down design. Each level (quarks to hadrons
to atoms to molecules to life) is an emergent phenomenon arising from
levels below (see Ch. 21 for the formal treatment of consciousness as
emergent self-organization).

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, leftrule=.75mm, title=\textcolor{quarto-callout-tip-color}{\faLightbulb}\hspace{0.5em}{Related Topics}, bottomrule=.15mm, opacityback=0, coltitle=black, arc=.35mm, colframe=quarto-callout-tip-color-frame, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-tip-color!10!white, colback=white, titlerule=0mm, breakable, bottomtitle=1mm, toptitle=1mm, rightrule=.15mm, toprule=.15mm, left=2mm, opacitybacktitle=0.6]

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Information and entropy}:
  Chapter~\ref{sec-information-entropy} formalizes what ``order'' means
\item
  \textbf{Complexity}: Chapter~\ref{sec-complexity} explores the
  emergence of complex structures
\item
  \textbf{The anthropic window}: Chapter~\ref{sec-anthropic-window} asks
  why constants permit complex observers
\item
  \textbf{Heat death}: Chapter~\ref{sec-heat-death} describes entropy's
  ultimate victory
\end{itemize}

\end{tcolorbox}

\chapter{The Hierarchy of Sentience}\label{sec-hierarchy-sentience}

\emph{``From molecule to mind, a continuous ladder''}

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, leftrule=.75mm, title=\textcolor{quarto-callout-note-color}{\faInfo}\hspace{0.5em}{Purpose of This Chapter}, bottomrule=.15mm, opacityback=0, coltitle=black, arc=.35mm, colframe=quarto-callout-note-color-frame, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-note-color!10!white, colback=white, titlerule=0mm, breakable, bottomtitle=1mm, toptitle=1mm, rightrule=.15mm, toprule=.15mm, left=2mm, opacitybacktitle=0.6]

This chapter establishes a \textbf{graded hierarchy of detection,
response, and awareness} from the simplest molecular systems to
conscious beings. We show that sentience is not binary but a spectrum,
with each level building on the capacities of those below.

\end{tcolorbox}

\section{The Continuity Principle}\label{the-continuity-principle}

Life and consciousness did not appear suddenly. They emerged through
gradual accumulation of capacities:

\[\text{Detection} \to \text{Response} \to \text{Memory} \to \text{Learning} \to \text{Modeling} \to \text{Self-Modeling} \to \text{Meta-Modeling}\]

Each transition adds computational capacity without sharp discontinuity.

\section{Level 0: Pure Physics (No
Detection)}\label{level-0-pure-physics-no-detection}

\subsection{Characterization}\label{characterization}

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}ll@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
Property & Value \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
Gates & 0 \\
Substrate & Any non-living matter \\
Capability & None (pure physical law) \\
Example & Rock, water molecule, star \\
\end{longtable}

\subsection{What Happens}\label{what-happens}

Physical systems evolve according to dynamics: - Forces act - States
change - No representation occurs

A rock does not ``detect'' being hit. The impact causes physical changes
(deformation, heating), but there is no internal state that
\emph{represents} the impact.

\subsection{FTD Formalism}\label{ftd-formalism}

\[s(v, t+1) = F(s(v,t), \mathbf{J}(v,t), N_{26}(v))\]

Pure update rule. No model, no memory, no detection.

\section{Level 1: Molecular
Detection}\label{level-1-molecular-detection}

\subsection{Characterization}\label{characterization-1}

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}ll@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
Property & Value \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
Gates & \textasciitilde1 (threshold detection) \\
Substrate & Responsive molecules \\
Capability & State change upon stimulus \\
Example & Enzyme, receptor protein, ion channel \\
\end{longtable}

\subsection{What Happens}\label{what-happens-1}

Certain molecular configurations change state when they encounter
specific inputs. A ligand arrives, triggers a conformational change in
the receptor, producing a new molecular state (active or inactive).
There is no persistent memory: the receptor resets when the ligand
leaves.

\subsection{Examples}\label{examples-5}

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}lll@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
Molecule & Detects & Response \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
Hemoglobin & \(\text{O}_2\) & Conformational change, binding \\
Ion channel & Voltage & Opens/closes \\
Enzyme & Substrate & Catalytic activity \\
Receptor & Ligand & Signal cascade initiation \\
\end{longtable}

\subsection{The Key Threshold}\label{the-key-threshold}

This is the \textbf{first level of detection}: a system whose state
change \emph{correlates} with an external condition. The molecule does
not ``know'' about the ligand, but its state carries information about
ligand presence.

\section{Level 2: Genetic
Information}\label{level-2-genetic-information}

\subsection{Characterization}\label{characterization-2}

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}ll@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
Property & Value \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
Gates & 1-2 (detection + storage) \\
Substrate & DNA/RNA \\
Capability & Hereditary memory, template replication \\
Example & DNA sequence, viral genome \\
\end{longtable}

\subsection{What Happens}\label{what-happens-2}

Genetic material stores information across time through four key
processes: storage in the nucleotide sequence, reading via transcription
(DNA → RNA), execution through translation (RNA → Protein), and copying
via replication (DNA → DNA).

\subsection{The Innovation}\label{the-innovation}

\textbf{Temporal persistence of information.} Unlike Level 1, where
detection is transient, genetic information persists indefinitely and
can be copied.

This is the birth of \textbf{hereditary memory}: information that
survives the death of the container.

\subsection{Information Content}\label{information-content}

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}lll@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
Genome & Base pairs & Bits (\textasciitilde2 per bp) \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
Virus & \(10^3\)-\(10^5\) & 2,000-200,000 \\
Bacterium & \(10^6\)-\(10^7\) & 2-20 million \\
Human & \(3 \times 10^9\) & 6 billion \\
\end{longtable}

\section{Level 3: Regulatory
Networks}\label{level-3-regulatory-networks}

\subsection{Characterization}\label{characterization-3}

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}ll@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
Property & Value \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
Gates & ≥2 (inference possible) \\
Substrate & Gene regulatory networks \\
Capability & Conditional response, feedback \\
Example & Lac operon, cell cycle control \\
\end{longtable}

\subsection{What Happens}\label{what-happens-3}

Genes regulate other genes, creating logical circuits. For example, in
the lac operon: if lactose is present AND glucose is absent, then
lactase genes are expressed; otherwise, they remain repressed.

\subsection{The Innovation}\label{the-innovation-1}

\textbf{Conditional logic.} The system's response depends on multiple
inputs combined according to rules. This is the first level of
\textbf{inference}: deriving appropriate action from combined evidence.

\subsection{Circuit Types}\label{circuit-types}

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}lll@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
Circuit & Function & Example \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
AND gate & Both inputs required & Lac operon \\
OR gate & Either input sufficient & Stress response \\
NOT gate & Inhibition & Repressor proteins \\
Feedback & Self-regulation & Cell cycle checkpoints \\
\end{longtable}

\section{Level 4: Cellular Sensing}\label{level-4-cellular-sensing}

\subsection{Characterization}\label{characterization-4}

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}ll@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
Property & Value \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
Gates & Many (complex integration) \\
Substrate & Single cell \\
Capability & Environmental sensing, chemotaxis \\
Example & Bacterium, amoeba \\
\end{longtable}

\subsection{What Happens}\label{what-happens-4}

The entire cell acts as an integrated sensing system. Inputs include
chemical gradients, light, and temperature. These are processed through
signal transduction cascades, producing outputs such as movement, gene
expression, or division. Short-term memory exists in the form of
adaptation mechanisms.

\subsection{Examples}\label{examples-6}

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}lll@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
Organism & Sense & Response \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
E. coli & Chemical gradient & Swim toward nutrients \\
Paramecium & Touch & Reversal, avoidance \\
Euglena & Light & Phototaxis \\
Amoeba & Prey presence & Engulfment \\
\end{longtable}

\subsection{The Innovation}\label{the-innovation-2}

\textbf{Integrated world-model.} The cell maintains a representation of
its environment sufficient for adaptive behavior. This is not
consciousness, but it is the first \textbf{proto-model} of external
reality.

\section{Level 5: Neural Networks
(Simple)}\label{level-5-neural-networks-simple}

\subsection{Characterization}\label{characterization-5}

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}ll@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
Property & Value \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
Gates & \(10^2\)-\(10^4\) \\
Substrate & Simple nervous system \\
Capability & Reflexes, habituation, sensitization \\
Example & Jellyfish, C. elegans \\
\end{longtable}

\subsection{What Happens}\label{what-happens-5}

Dedicated cells (neurons) specialize in information processing. Sensory
neurons detect stimuli, interneurons integrate signals, and motor
neurons drive behavior. Learning occurs through synaptic modification.

\subsection{C. elegans: The Minimal Nervous
System}\label{c.-elegans-the-minimal-nervous-system}

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}ll@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
Property & Value \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
Neurons & 302 (exactly) \\
Synapses & \textasciitilde7,000 \\
Behaviors & Chemotaxis, thermotaxis, touch response \\
Learning & Habituation, classical conditioning \\
\end{longtable}

\subsection{The Innovation}\label{the-innovation-3}

\textbf{Dedicated computational substrate.} Neurons are optimized for
rapid information transmission and integration. This enables faster,
more complex responses than chemical signaling alone.

\section{Level 6: Centralized
Processing}\label{level-6-centralized-processing}

\subsection{Characterization}\label{characterization-6}

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}ll@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
Property & Value \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
Gates & \(10^4\)-\(10^6\) \\
Substrate & Ganglia, simple brain \\
Capability & Coordinated behavior, basic learning \\
Example & Insects, mollusks \\
\end{longtable}

\subsection{What Happens}\label{what-happens-6}

Neural processing becomes centralized. Specialized sensory organs (eyes,
antennae) gather information. The brain coordinates all processing.
Motor systems execute coordinated movement patterns. Memory includes
spatial maps and learned associations.

\subsection{Capabilities}\label{capabilities}

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}lll@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
Organism & Neurons & Capabilities \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
Honeybee & 960,000 & Navigation, communication, learning \\
Octopus & 500 million & Problem-solving, tool use, play \\
Jumping spider & 600,000 & Planning, deception \\
\end{longtable}

\subsection{The Innovation}\label{the-innovation-4}

\textbf{World-models with spatial structure.} These organisms maintain
internal maps of their environment: representations of where things are
and how to navigate.

\section{Level 7: Vertebrate Brains}\label{level-7-vertebrate-brains}

\subsection{Characterization}\label{characterization-7}

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}ll@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
Property & Value \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
Gates & \(10^6\)-\(10^{11}\) \\
Substrate & Vertebrate brain \\
Capability & Emotion, social cognition, flexible learning \\
Example & Fish, reptiles, birds, mammals \\
\end{longtable}

\subsection{The Limbic Innovation}\label{the-limbic-innovation}

Vertebrates add \textbf{emotional processing} through specialized
structures: the amygdala for threat detection and fear, the hippocampus
for episodic memory and spatial maps, the hypothalamus for homeostatic
drives, and the reward system for reinforcement learning.

\subsection{Capabilities by Group}\label{capabilities-by-group}

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}ll@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
Group & Key Innovation \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
Fish & Spatial learning, social hierarchies \\
Reptiles & Territorial behavior, basic problem-solving \\
Birds & Tool use, vocal learning, planning \\
Mammals & Extended parental care, play, social learning \\
\end{longtable}

\subsection{The Innovation}\label{the-innovation-5}

\textbf{Self-models emerge.} Vertebrates show evidence of body schema:
internal representations of their own physical structure. This is the
beginning of \textbf{self-awareness} in the minimal sense.

\section{Level 8: Primate Cognition}\label{level-8-primate-cognition}

\subsection{Characterization}\label{characterization-8}

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}ll@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
Property & Value \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
Gates & \(10^{10}\)-\(10^{11}\) \\
Substrate & Primate neocortex \\
Capability & Theory of mind, deception, culture \\
Example & Monkeys, apes \\
\end{longtable}

\subsection{Theory of Mind}\label{theory-of-mind}

The capacity to model \emph{other minds} operates at multiple levels:
Level 0 (I want X), Level 1 (I know that you want Y), Level 2 (I know
that you think I want X), and Level 3 (I know that you think I think you
want Y).

\subsection{Evidence}\label{evidence-1}

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}ll@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
Behavior & Implies \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
Deceptive pointing & Models others' beliefs \\
Gaze following & Tracks others' attention \\
Tactical deception & Manipulates others' models \\
Consolation & Models others' emotional states \\
\end{longtable}

\subsection{The Innovation}\label{the-innovation-6}

\textbf{Meta-modeling of others.} Primates can represent not just the
world, but other beings' representations of the world. This is
\textbf{social intelligence}: the precursor to full self-consciousness.

\section{Level 9: Human
Consciousness}\label{level-9-human-consciousness}

\subsection{Characterization}\label{characterization-9}

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}ll@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
Property & Value \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
Gates & \textasciitilde{}\(10^{11}\) \\
Substrate & Human brain \\
Capability & Language, abstract reasoning, recursive self-reflection \\
Example & Homo sapiens \\
\end{longtable}

\subsection{The Linguistic Innovation}\label{the-linguistic-innovation}

Language enables: symbols (arbitrary signs for concepts),
compositionality (infinite combinations from finite parts), displacement
(reference to absent or abstract entities), and meta-language (language
about language).

\subsection{Recursive Self-Reference}\label{recursive-self-reference}

Humans can engage in unlimited meta-levels:

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}ll@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
Level & Example \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
0 & I see a tree \\
1 & I know I see a tree \\
2 & I know that I know I see a tree \\
3 & I wonder why I know that I know\ldots{} \\
n & (Potentially unlimited) \\
\end{longtable}

\subsection{The Innovation}\label{the-innovation-7}

\textbf{Full meta-sLoop.} Humans have meta-models of their own
meta-models, enabling: - Philosophy (thinking about thinking) -
Self-improvement (modeling and modifying oneself) - Existential
awareness (knowing that one will die)

\section{The Complete Hierarchy}\label{the-complete-hierarchy}

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, leftrule=.75mm, title=\textcolor{quarto-callout-important-color}{\faExclamation}\hspace{0.5em}{The Ladder of Sentience}, bottomrule=.15mm, opacityback=0, coltitle=black, arc=.35mm, colframe=quarto-callout-important-color-frame, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-important-color!10!white, colback=white, titlerule=0mm, breakable, bottomtitle=1mm, toptitle=1mm, rightrule=.15mm, toprule=.15mm, left=2mm, opacitybacktitle=0.6]

  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 8\tabcolsep) * \real{0.2000}}@{}}

\begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright

\section{Transitions Are Gradual}\label{transitions-are-gradual}

No sharp boundary separates these levels:

\subsection{Evidence for Continuity}\label{evidence-for-continuity}

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}lll@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
Capacity & Once thought unique to & Now found in \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
Tool use & Humans & Crows, octopi, chimps \\
Language & Humans & Bees (limited), apes (limited) \\
Self-recognition & Humans & Elephants, dolphins, magpies \\
Planning & Humans & Scrub jays, great apes \\
Culture & Humans & Chimps, whales, crows \\
\end{longtable}

\subsection{The Implication}\label{the-implication}

Consciousness is not a magic property that appears at some threshold. It
is the \textbf{high end of a continuous spectrum} of increasingly
sophisticated information processing.

\section{FTD Formalization}\label{ftd-formalization}

\subsection{Detection Threshold}\label{detection-threshold}

A system at level \(n\) can be characterized by:

\[D_n = \log_2(\text{distinguishable states})\]

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}ll@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
Level & Approximate \(D_n\) \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
0 & 0 \\
1 & 1-10 \\
2 & \(10^3\)-\(10^7\) \\
3 & \(10^2\)-\(10^3\) \\
4 & \(10^3\)-\(10^4\) \\
5-9 & \(\propto\) neuron count \\
\end{longtable}

\subsection{Self-Model Complexity}\label{self-model-complexity}

The complexity of the self-model \(\phi(\Omega)\) scales with level:

\[C(\phi) \approx k \cdot \text{Gates}^{\beta}\]

where \(\beta < 1\) due to compression (efficient representation).

\subsection{Meta-Level Depth}\label{meta-level-depth}

Maximum recursion depth \(R\) of self-reference:

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}ll@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
Level & \(R\) \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
0-4 & 0 \\
5-6 & 0-1 \\
7 & 1-2 \\
8 & 2-3 \\
9 & Unlimited (language-enabled) \\
\end{longtable}

\section{Implications}\label{implications-3}

\subsection{For Artificial
Intelligence}\label{for-artificial-intelligence}

Current AI systems: - Excel at Level 3-4 tasks (pattern recognition,
optimization) - Approach Level 5-6 (learning, generalization) - Lack
Levels 7+ (genuine self-model, embodiment)

\subsection{For Animal Ethics}\label{for-animal-ethics}

If sentience is graded, moral consideration should be too: - Level 1-3:
No sentience, no moral status - Level 4-5: Minimal sentience, minimal
consideration - Level 6+: Significant sentience, significant
consideration - Level 7+: Self-awareness, strong moral status

\subsection{For the Definition of
Life}\label{for-the-definition-of-life}

From Ch. 12.0, life requires self-model + feedback. This corresponds to:
- Minimum: Level 2 (genetic memory) - Functional: Level 3+ (regulatory
feedback)

Life is the \textbf{platform} for higher levels of sentience, not
identical to them.

\section{Concepts}\label{concepts-38}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{sentience-hierarchy}: Graded levels from detection to
  consciousness
\item
  \textbf{detection-threshold}: Minimum stimulus for state change
\item
  \textbf{world-model}: Internal representation of environment
\item
  \textbf{self-model}: Internal representation of own structure
\item
  \textbf{meta-modeling}: Representation of representations
\item
  \textbf{theory-of-mind}: Modeling others' mental states
\item
  \textbf{recursive-self-reference}: Self-models that include the
  self-model itself
\item
  \textbf{continuity-principle}: No sharp boundary between levels
\item
  \textbf{gate-count}: Computational capacity measure
\end{itemize}

\section{Transition}\label{transition-39}

With the hierarchy of sentience established, we now have the conceptual
framework to understand consciousness not as a mysterious emergence but
as the apex of a continuous ladder: the level at which self-modeling
becomes self-modeling of self-modeling. The next chapter examines this
apex: consciousness as meta-sLoop (see Ch. 21 for the formal treatment).

\chapter{Information and Entropy}\label{sec-information-entropy}

\emph{``The arrow of time''}

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, leftrule=.75mm, title=\textcolor{quarto-callout-note-color}{\faInfo}\hspace{0.5em}{Key Revelation}, bottomrule=.15mm, opacityback=0, coltitle=black, arc=.35mm, colframe=quarto-callout-note-color-frame, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-note-color!10!white, colback=white, titlerule=0mm, breakable, bottomtitle=1mm, toptitle=1mm, rightrule=.15mm, toprule=.15mm, left=2mm, opacitybacktitle=0.6]

Information is physical. Erasing a bit releases heat. Entropy measures
what we do not know. The second law drives the universe toward
equilibrium: but pockets of order can persist, borrowing from the cosmic
entropy budget.

\end{tcolorbox}

\section{Shannon Entropy}\label{shannon-entropy}

\subsection{Measuring Uncertainty}\label{measuring-uncertainty}

How surprised are you by a message? \[
H = -\sum_i p_i \log_2(p_i)
\]

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}lll@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
Outcome & Probability & Surprise \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
Certain (p=1) & Always & 0 bits \\
Coin flip (p=0.5) & Random & 1 bit \\
Die roll (p=1/6) & More random & 2.58 bits \\
\end{longtable}

\subsection{Information Content}\label{information-content-1}

A message carries information equal to the entropy it reduces: \[
I = H_{before} - H_{after}
\]

Learning the coin landed heads: 1 bit of information.

\section{Thermodynamic Entropy}\label{thermodynamic-entropy}

\subsection{Boltzmann's Formula}\label{boltzmanns-formula}

\[
S = k_B \ln(\Omega)
\]

Where Ω = number of microstates consistent with macrostate.

\subsection{Example: Gas Expansion}\label{example-gas-expansion}

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}lll@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
State & Microstates & Entropy \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
Left half only & 2\^{}N / 2\^{}N = 1 & 0 \\
Full volume & 2\^{}N & N k\_B ln(2) \\
\end{longtable}

Entropy increases when constraint removed.

\section{Von Neumann Entropy}\label{von-neumann-entropy}

\subsection{Quantum Information}\label{quantum-information}

For quantum systems, Shannon entropy is insufficient. We need the
\textbf{von Neumann entropy}: \[
S_{vN} = -\text{Tr}(\rho \ln \rho)
\]

where \(\rho\) is the \textbf{density matrix} describing the quantum
state.

\subsection{Density Matrix in FTD}\label{density-matrix-in-ftd}

In the FTD Hilbert space H\_TRD = L²(Lattice, ℂ), the density matrix for
a pure state \textbar ψ⟩ is: \[
\rho = |\psi\rangle\langle\psi|
\]

For mixed states (statistical ensembles of pure states): \[
\rho = \sum_i p_i |\psi_i\rangle\langle\psi_i|
\]

\subsection{Properties}\label{properties-22}

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}lll@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
Property & Pure State & Mixed State \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
S\_vN & 0 & \textgreater{} 0 \\
Tr(ρ²) & 1 & \textless{} 1 \\
Interpretation & Complete knowledge & Incomplete knowledge \\
\end{longtable}

\subsection{Entanglement Entropy}\label{entanglement-entropy}

For entangled systems A ⊗ B, the \textbf{entanglement entropy} measures
quantum correlations: \[
S_A = -\text{Tr}(\rho_A \ln \rho_A)
\]

where \(\rho_A = \text{Tr}_B(\rho_{AB})\) is the reduced density matrix.

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, leftrule=.75mm, title=\textcolor{quarto-callout-note-color}{\faInfo}\hspace{0.5em}{FTD Connection}, bottomrule=.15mm, opacityback=0, coltitle=black, arc=.35mm, colframe=quarto-callout-note-color-frame, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-note-color!10!white, colback=white, titlerule=0mm, breakable, bottomtitle=1mm, toptitle=1mm, rightrule=.15mm, toprule=.15mm, left=2mm, opacitybacktitle=0.6]

In FTD, entangled pairs created via pair production have: - \textbf{Pure
total state}: S\_vN(AB) = 0 - \textbf{Mixed subsystems}: S\_vN(A) =
S\_vN(B) = ln(2)

This matches the quantum prediction for maximally entangled Bell states.

\end{tcolorbox}

\subsection{Area Law}\label{area-law}

For ground states of local Hamiltonians, entanglement entropy scales
with boundary area: \[
S_A \propto \frac{\text{Area}(\partial A)}{l_P^2}
\]

This connects to the holographic principle and black hole entropy.

\section{The Connection}\label{the-connection}

\subsection{Landauer's Principle}\label{landauers-principle}

Erasing one bit of information: \[
E_{min} = k_B T \ln(2)
\]

At room temperature: \textasciitilde{}\(3 \times 10^{-21}\) J per bit.

Information is physical. Computation has thermodynamic cost.

Resolution)}\label{maxwells-demon-standard-resolution}

\section{The Second Law}\label{the-second-law-1}

\subsection{Statement}\label{statement}

Total entropy of isolated system never decreases: \[
dS_{total} \geq 0
\]

\subsection{Why?}\label{why-1}

Not a fundamental law: a statistical truth: - Overwhelmingly more
disordered states than ordered - Random evolution → most likely state -
Most likely = highest entropy

\section{The Arrow of Time}\label{the-arrow-of-time}

\subsection{Past vs Future}\label{past-vs-future}

Why do we remember the past, not the future?

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}lll@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
Direction & Entropy & Memories \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
Past & Lower & Recorded \\
Future & Higher & Unknown \\
\end{longtable}

Memory requires entropy increase in brain.

\subsection{The Past Hypothesis}\label{the-past-hypothesis}

Universe started in extraordinarily low entropy state: - Big Bang:
Smooth, uniform - Today: Clumpy, complex - Heat death: Smooth again

\subsection{In FTD Terms}\label{in-ftd-terms}

The universe begins in the all-Void state (s = 0 everywhere), the
minimum entropy configuration. As time progresses, genesis creates
particles that clump and form structures, increasing local complexity
while global entropy rises. Eventually the universe returns toward void,
approaching maximum entropy in the heat death.

\subsection{The Boundary Condition
Solution}\label{the-boundary-condition-solution}

Why did the universe start with low entropy? In FTD, the answer is
\textbf{boundary conditions on the action principle}.

The FTD action:

\[S[s,J] = \sum_t \sum_v \mathcal{L}(s,J)\]

requires boundary conditions at both temporal ends. Variational
principles need to know \textbf{where to start and where to end}.

\textbf{Initial condition}: The simplest boundary is all-Void:

\[s(v, t=0) = 0 \quad \forall v\]

This is the \textbf{unique maximum-symmetry state}: homogeneous,
isotropic, zero information content.

\textbf{Final condition}: The action principle requires specifying the
endpoint. In cosmology, this is the \textbf{heat death} state:

\[\lim_{t \to \infty} S(t) = S_{max}\]

Maximum entropy, uniform distribution, no gradients.

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, leftrule=.75mm, title=\textcolor{quarto-callout-important-color}{\faExclamation}\hspace{0.5em}{The Arrow is Geometric}, bottomrule=.15mm, opacityback=0, coltitle=black, arc=.35mm, colframe=quarto-callout-important-color-frame, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-important-color!10!white, colback=white, titlerule=0mm, breakable, bottomtitle=1mm, toptitle=1mm, rightrule=.15mm, toprule=.15mm, left=2mm, opacitybacktitle=0.6]

The arrow of time is not a mystery to be explained: it is a
\textbf{boundary condition} of the variational problem. Time's direction
is the direction from low-entropy boundary to high-entropy boundary.

\end{tcolorbox}

\subsection{Why This Boundary?}\label{why-this-boundary}

Why these particular boundary conditions? Three answers:

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\item
  \textbf{Anthropic}: Only universes with low initial entropy produce
  observers. We could not be asking this question otherwise.
\item
  \textbf{Uniqueness}: The all-Void state is the \textbf{unique} state
  with maximum symmetry. Any other initial condition requires specifying
  additional structure: breaking symmetry without explanation.
\item
  \textbf{Self-consistency}: The action principle itself requires smooth
  boundaries. The all-Void → heat-death trajectory is the smoothest
  possible evolution.
\end{enumerate}

\subsection{Retrocausality and the Block
Universe}\label{retrocausality-and-the-block-universe}

In FTD, the action principle is \textbf{non-local in time}. The path
that extremizes S depends on both initial and final conditions.

This does not mean the future causes the past. It means:

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}ll@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
View & Interpretation \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
\textbf{Process} & Universe evolves tick-by-tick, following rules \\
\textbf{Block} & Entire 4D history extremizes action \\
\end{longtable}

Both views are valid. The rules are local; the explanation is global.

\subsection{Information is Conserved}\label{information-is-conserved}

The flux field evolution is \textbf{unitary}: information is never
created or destroyed, only redistributed.

\[I_{total}(t) = I_{total}(0) = \text{constant}\]

Entropy increases because information spreads out, not because it
disappears. The distinction matters for:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Black hole information paradox}: Information escapes via
  Hawking radiation correlations
\item
  \textbf{Quantum mechanics}: Unitarity preserved at the fundamental
  level
\item
  \textbf{Reversibility}: In principle, the simulation could run
  backward
\end{itemize}

\[

\]

Systems minimize free energy, not just energy.

\subsection{Life and Free Energy}\label{life-and-free-energy}

Living systems: - Import low-entropy energy (sunlight, food) - Export
high-entropy waste (heat) - Maintain local order by increasing global
entropy

Paradox}\label{black-hole-information-paradox}

If information falls into black hole: - Hawking radiation is thermal (no
information) - Information seemingly destroyed - Violates quantum
mechanics

Resolution: Information encoded in subtle correlations (still debated).

\subsection{Holographic Principle}\label{holographic-principle}

Maximum information in region: \[
I_{max} = \frac{A}{4 l_P^2}
\]

Proportional to surface area, not volume!

\section{Societal Noetics: Information as
Perturbation}\label{societal-noetics-information-as-perturbation}

The principles of entropy and information extend to the societal level.

\subsection{Cultural Objectivity}\label{cultural-objectivity}

Societies maintain a \textbf{Shared Epistemic Model}
(\(\Phi_{culture}\)) based on accumulated information. This ``Cultural
Objectivity'' behaves like a physical object with \textbf{Noetic
Inertia}: it resists change.

\subsection{Propaganda as
Perturbation}\label{propaganda-as-perturbation}

``Propaganda'' is the injection of \textbf{Informational Entropy}
designed to destabilize \(\Phi_{culture}\). 1. \textbf{Perturbation:}
High-intensity flux (narrative) disrupts the existing coherence of the
cultural model. 2. \textbf{Restructuring:} The model re-condenses around
the new attractor (the propagated truth). 3. \textbf{The Limit:} This
manipulates the \textbf{Epistemic Domain} (meaning), but cannot alter
the \textbf{Ontic Domain} (physics). No amount of propaganda can change
the value of \(G^*\).

This distinguishes \textbf{Political Truth} (mutable/informational) from
\textbf{Physical Truth} (immutable/geometric).

\section{In the Simulation}\label{in-the-simulation}

\subsection{Tracking Entropy}\label{tracking-entropy}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ compute\_entropy(grid):}
    \CommentTok{\# Partition into cells}
\NormalTok{    cells }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ partition(grid, CELL\_SIZE)}

    \CommentTok{\# Count particles per cell}
\NormalTok{    counts }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ [}\BuiltInTok{len}\NormalTok{(c.particles) }\ControlFlowTok{for}\NormalTok{ c }\KeywordTok{in}\NormalTok{ cells]}

    \CommentTok{\# Compute probability distribution}
\NormalTok{    total }\OperatorTok{=} \BuiltInTok{sum}\NormalTok{(counts)}
\NormalTok{    probs }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ [c}\OperatorTok{/}\NormalTok{total }\ControlFlowTok{for}\NormalTok{ c }\KeywordTok{in}\NormalTok{ counts }\ControlFlowTok{if}\NormalTok{ c }\OperatorTok{\textgreater{}} \DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{]}

    \CommentTok{\# Shannon entropy}
\NormalTok{    H }\OperatorTok{=} \OperatorTok{{-}}\BuiltInTok{sum}\NormalTok{(p }\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ log2(p) }\ControlFlowTok{for}\NormalTok{ p }\KeywordTok{in}\NormalTok{ probs)}

    \ControlFlowTok{return}\NormalTok{ H}

\KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ track\_arrow\_of\_time(grid, history):}
\NormalTok{    H\_now }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ compute\_entropy(grid)}
\NormalTok{    history.append(H\_now)}

    \CommentTok{\# Verify second law (statistically)}
    \ControlFlowTok{if} \BuiltInTok{len}\NormalTok{(history) }\OperatorTok{\textgreater{}} \DecValTok{100}\NormalTok{:}
\NormalTok{        trend }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ linear\_fit(history[}\OperatorTok{{-}}\DecValTok{100}\NormalTok{:])}
        \ControlFlowTok{assert}\NormalTok{ trend.slope }\OperatorTok{\textgreater{}=} \DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{, }\StringTok{"Second law violated!"}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

Despite entropy increase, information is conserved: - UUID tracks every
particle - Entanglement partners remembered - Total flux conserved -
History could be reversed (in principle)

\section{Concepts}\label{concepts-39}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{shannon-entropy}: Measure of uncertainty in classical
  information
\item
  \textbf{boltzmann-entropy}: Thermodynamic entropy from microstate
  counting
\item
  \textbf{von-neumann-entropy}: Quantum entropy: S = -Tr(ρ ln ρ)
\item
  \textbf{density-matrix}: Quantum state representation for pure and
  mixed states
\item
  \textbf{entanglement-entropy}: Measures quantum correlations in
  bipartite systems
\item
  \textbf{landauer-principle}: Information erasure costs energy
\item
  \textbf{arrow-of-time}: Entropy's temporal asymmetry
\item
  \textbf{purity}: Tr(ρ²), distinguishes pure (=1) from mixed
  (\textless1) states
\end{itemize}

\section{Measures of Complexity}\label{measures-of-complexity}

\subsection{Kolmogorov Complexity}\label{kolmogorov-complexity}

Length of shortest program to produce output: \[
K(x) = \min_p \{ |p| : U(p) = x \}
\]

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}lll@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
Pattern & K & Type \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
000\ldots0 & Small & Simple \\
Random & \(\approx\) Length & Incompressible \\
\(\pi\) digits & Small & Compressible (has program) \\
Life & Intermediate & Complex \\
\end{longtable}

\subsection{The Mandelbrot Set: Complexity from
Simplicity}\label{the-mandelbrot-set-complexity-from-simplicity}

The Mandelbrot set is perhaps the purest example of complexity emerging
from simplicity. The entire infinite structure is defined by a single
iteration:

\[z_{n+1} = z_n^2 + c\]

This two-symbol formula has Kolmogorov complexity of approximately 50
bits. Yet it generates:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Infinite boundary length (fractal dimension ≈ 2)
\item
  Self-similar structure at all scales
\item
  Every possible pattern encoded somewhere in the boundary
\end{itemize}

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}ll@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
Mandelbrot Property & Complexity Implication \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
Formula: \textasciitilde50 bits & Extremely low K(program) \\
Boundary: infinite detail & Infinite \(K\)(output) \\
Computation: unbounded & High logical depth \\
Self-similarity & High effective complexity \\
\end{longtable}

The Mandelbrot set demonstrates that \textbf{complexity is not about
complicated rules}. The simplest self-referential iteration, applied
repeatedly, generates infinite structure. This is the paradigm for how
consciousness emerges from the sLoop: not through elaborate mechanisms
but through the power of self-reference.

}

\end{figure}%

\subsection{Logical Depth}\label{logical-depth}

Time required for shortest program to run: - Simple: Short program,
quick execution - Random: No program captures it - Complex: Short
program, long execution

Life is logically deep.

\subsection{Effective Complexity}\label{effective-complexity}

Amount of regularity: \[
C_{eff} = K(regularities)
\]

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Random: Low effective complexity (no regularities)
\item
  Simple: Low effective complexity (few regularities)
\item
  Complex: High effective complexity (many regularities)
\end{itemize}

\section{The Edge of Chaos Revisited}\label{the-edge-of-chaos-revisited}

\subsection{The Mandelbrot Boundary as Edge of
Chaos}\label{the-mandelbrot-boundary-as-edge-of-chaos}

The Mandelbrot set provides the clearest visualization of the edge of
chaos:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Inside the set (ordered)}: Orbits converge to fixed points or
  cycles. Predictable, stable, but computationally trivial.
\item
  \textbf{Outside the set (chaotic)}: Orbits escape to infinity. No
  structure, no memory, no computation.
\item
  \textbf{At the boundary (critical)}: Orbits neither converge nor
  escape. This is where all the complexity lives.
\end{itemize}

The entire infinitely complex fractal boundary is the \emph{edge}
between order and chaos. This is not coincidence. The boundary is
exactly where the iteration is ``deciding'' whether to persist or
dissolve, whether to remain bounded or escape.

\textbf{This is the locus of consciousness}: not in the stable interior
(unconscious automation) nor in the chaotic exterior (no coherent
process) but at the critical boundary where the sLoop perpetually
balances between persistence and dissolution.

Computation}\label{phase-transitions-in-computation}
